State of the Market 2023 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

1  EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 

The major story in 2023 was natural gas prices returning to a more normal range after reaching 

extraordinarily high prices in 2022.  This decrease in gas prices has a far-ranging impact on 

many of the metrics in this report.  Many of the themes that have been identified in previous 

years – increasing renewable generation and resource adequacy challenges – continue in 2023.  

Wind generation continues to play an increasing role in SPP’s markets.  This has produced many 

challenges including increasing variability and uncertainty of supply, out-of-market actions to 

ensure system reliability, higher make-whole payments, and negative prices.   

Some key observations in the SPP Integrated Marketplace during 2023: 

•  Gas prices decreased.  The average gas price for 2023 at the Panhandle Eastern hub was 

$2.16/MMBtu, a decrease of 63 percent from 2022 when the average price was 

$5.83/MMBtu. 

•  Day-ahead market and real-time market prices decreased.  The average day-ahead 

price for 2023 was $26/MWh, a decrease of 46 percent from $48/MWh in 2022.  The 

average real-time price was $24/MWh for 2023, a decrease of 47 percent from $44/MWh 

in 2022.  Lower gas prices in 2023 are the largest contributor for the decrease.  

•  Revenue neutrality uplift decreased.  Revenue neutrality uplift dropped sharply from 

2022 and was even below 2021 levels.  Revenue neutrality uplift for 2023 was $205 

million, down just over 62 percent from $546 million in 2022 and $285 million in 2021.  

Most of the decrease can be attributed to a decrease in the real-time congestion 

component, which was down nearly 57 percent from $654 million in 2022 to $283 million 

in 2023. 

•  Make-whole payments decreased.  Day-ahead make-whole payments declined from 

$173 million in 2022 to $79 million in 2023, a decrease of over 54 percent.  Likewise, 

reliability unit commitment make-whole payments were down nearly 47 percent, from 

$292 million in 2022 to $157 million in 2023.  Much of these decreases can be attributed 

to the decrease in natural gas prices.   

•  Addition of wind resources has slowed.  In 2023, just under 1,700 MW nameplate 

capacity of new wind resources was added to the market.  During 2022, 1,500 MW of 

nameplate wind capacity was added.  The total capacity added over the last two years 

equals the amount of nameplate capacity added in 2021, which was 3,200 MW.  

State of the Market 2023 

1 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

However, the generation interconnection queue still contains a large amount of new 

wind resources to potentially be added to the market, along with a growing amount of 

solar and battery/storage resources. 

•  Wind penetration increased.  Installed nameplate wind capacity stood at 33,725 MW at 

the end of 2023.  Wind generation capacity now accounts for 34 percent of installed 

nameplate capacity in the SPP market. 

•  Generation mix continued to evolve.  For the second straight year, wind generation 

produced the highest percentage of total generation, at 37 percent in 2023.  Coal 

generation dropped from 33 percent of total generation in 2022 to 27 percent in 2023.  

With the lower gas prices during 2023, gas combined-cycle resources climbed from 13 

percent of total generation in 2022 to 17 percent in 2023. 

•  Negative price intervals decreased.  The frequency of negative price intervals decreased 

by 10 percent in the day-ahead market and decreased by 19 percent in the real-time 

market from 2022.  Six percent of all asset owner intervals in the day-ahead market had 

negative prices in 2023, down from seven percent in 2022.  In 2023, 12 percent of the 

real-time asset owner intervals had negative prices, down from 15 percent in 2022.   

•  Congestion costs decreased.  Total congestion payments for 2023 were $1.4 billion; 

down from $2.0 billion in 2022.  This decrease can primarily be attributed to a reduction 

in fuel supply costs. 

•  Transmission congestion rights funding fell outside of the targeted range.  For the 

second straight year, transmission congestion rights funding was at 88 percent, just 

below the target threshold of 90 percent.  Much of this shortfall was the result of 

unaccounted for outages in the congestion hedging model and mismatches between the 

congestion hedging model ratings and day-ahead market ratings.  However, the annual 

shortfall decreased by $112 million year-over-year.   

•  Demand response capacity increased.  During 2023, 56 dispatchable demand response 

resources, representing 449 MW of nameplate capacity, were added to the SPP market.  

Additionally, 64 demand response resources retired during the year, representing 25 MW 

of nameplate capacity.  At the end of 2023, there was 793 MW of dispatchable demand 

response resources of installed nameplate capacity. 

•  Market-to-market payments from MISO were down.  Net market-to-market payments 

from MISO to SPP for 2023 were $93 million, down nearly 42 percent from $160 million 

in 2022.

•  Virtual profits decreased.  Average profit per cleared virtual megawatt after fees was 

$1.10/MW in 2023, down just over 25 percent from $1.47/MW in 2022. 

•  Outaged capacity increased.  Total outages and derates, for capacity taken out-of-

service for maintenance, increased by six percent from 2022 to 2023 - slightly below 

2021 levels.  Forced outages and derates also had an increase of six percent from 2022 

to 2023. 

•  Energy consumption decreased.  Monthly average system energy consumption was 

down one-half percent compared to 2022.  Most of this decrease can be attributed to 

lessened weather impacts during 2023. 

•  Self-committed capacity decreased slightly.  The average percentage of total offered 

capacity by commitment status shows a two percentage point increase in market status 

and a one percentage point decrease in self-commitment status.   

•  Regional congestion patterns.  The areas that experienced the highest congestion costs 

in 2023 were the northwest corner of North Dakota, southeastern Oklahoma, and a 

concentrated area in southeast North Dakota near the MISO seam.  Much of Kansas, 

Nebraska, South Dakota, and northwest Oklahoma experienced the lowest congestion 

costs for the year. 

•  Overall, hedging covered congestion costs.  In aggregate, load-serving entities covered 

149 percent of their congestion cost and non-load-serving entities covered 66 percent of 

their total congestion cost. 

• 

Individual congesting hedging ranged in performance.  Individual market participants 

hedged congestion with varying degrees of effectiveness.  Overall, 75 percent of load-

serving entities recovered all their congestion costs. 

•  Auction revenue right funding decreased.  Auction revenue right funding decreased 

from 122 percent in 2022 to 118 percent in 2023 and its surplus decreased by more than 

$40 million year-over-year.

SPP markets have been competitive over the last several years.  The market share,

moderate potential structural market power in SPP markets outside of frequently 

constrained areas.

Executive summary 

•  Offer mitigation in day-ahead increased slightly but remains rare.  Incremental energy 

offer mitigation in 2023 slightly increased in frequency in the day-ahead market.  Despite 

the minor increase in mitigated resource hours, energy offer mitigation remains very 

rare, at 0.31 percent of resource hours in the day-ahead.  The total frequency of 

mitigation across all other products was similarly low and in line with prior years, with a 

slight uptick in spinning reserve mitigation concentrated in April, October, and 

November. 

•  Exercising market power remains rare.  Behavioral measures suggest that attempts to 

exercise market power by manipulating the price (economic withholding) or quantity 

(physical withholding) of generation are rare.  The output gap, an inference of 

economically withheld generation, was slightly lower in 2023.  Overall, it remains at very 

low levels, averaging less than 0.2 percent in all months.  This reflects a high level of 

participation in the market overall.

2021, December 2022, and January 2024 highlighted significant issues with SPP’s

operations have been declared during periods of mild weather when a combination of 

outages and a lower wind output have led to tight conditions.  Key issues with the 

current supply adequacy approach include a lack of a seasonal resource adequacy 

requirement, fuel availability risks, correlated output and outages among similar 

resources, and an accreditation process that does not reflect actual resource 

performance.

Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

1.1  OVERVIEW 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

The overarching theme throughout 2023 was the effect of lower gas prices on prices and costs 

during the year.  The average gas cost at the Panhandle hub decreased by 63 percent from 2022 

to 2023, which resulted in lower electricity prices, lower make-whole payments, lower 

congestion costs, and a myriad of other effects.  Although 2023 was bookended by extreme 

winter weather events in December 2022 and January 2024, there were no extreme weather 

conditions during 2023 that affected the market outside of a short stretch of well above average 

temperatures in late August. 

As with previous years, energy costs remain to be the largest component of total wholesale 

costs, which represented 96 percent of total costs in 2023.  Historically, the percentage has 

remained around 96 percent since the start of the Integrated Marketplace in 2014.  However, the 

2021 winter weather event was an outlier; make-whole payments reached over $1 billion, and 

the percentage decreased to 85 percent. 

The annual five-minute peak demand of 56,139 MW was five percent higher this year compared 

to last year, however, total electricity consumption was down only one-half percent.  Of the 

1,806 MW net increase in nameplate generation capacity from 2022, 1,693 MW were from wind 

resources, 431 MW were from dispatchable demand response, and 239 MW were from solar 
resources.1   

After climbing steadily, wind generation, as a percent of total generation, dropped from 38 

percent in 2022 to 37 percent in 2023.  After a rise from 2020 to 2021, coal generation 

continued to decrease, representing 27 percent of total generation in 2023; down from 33 

percent in 2022.  The increase in coal generation from 2020 to 2021 is primarily the result of 

higher gas prices in 2021, making coal generation more economic than some gas resources. 

1.2  DAY-AHEAD AND REAL-TIME MARKET PERFORMANCE 

Overall, both day-ahead and real-time energy prices were lower in 2023, compared to 2022.  The 

average hourly day-ahead price of $26/MWh in 2023 was nearly 46 percent lower than the 2022 

price of $48/MWh.  Likewise, the average hourly real-time price of $24/MWh in 2023 was 47 

percent lower than the 2022 price.  Much of this decrease can be attributed to the lower gas 

costs during 2023. 

1 The sum of the nameplate capacity of added resources in 2023 is higher than the net amount of 
nameplate capacity added due to retirements and derates of other resources in the market. 

State of the Market 2023 

5 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

As mentioned in the overview, gas prices were down in 2023, with a monthly average of 

$2.17/MMBtu, a decrease of nearly 63 percent from $5.83/MMBtu in 2023.  The monthly 

average gas price ranged from $2.82/MMBtu in January to $1.86/MMBtu in May. 

Load participation in the day-ahead market dropped slightly in 2023.  The average monthly level 

of participation for the load assets was between 96 percent and 100 percent of the actual real-

time load, with the annual average of just under 98 percent.  Prior to 2022, the monthly level 

averaged between 98 and 101 percent, with annual averages of 100 percent in 2020 and 99 

percent in 2021.   

On average for the year, wind generation was 2,750 MW higher in the real-time market 

compared to the amount scheduled in the day-ahead market on an hourly basis.  This 

represents a persisting and escalating challenge to the market, as wind generation continues its 

expansion. 

Virtual bids and offers may theoretically offset the under-scheduling of renewable supply in the 

day-ahead market.  However, in net they did not as they averaged 1,650 MW of net virtual 

supply.  Furthermore, it is important to recognize that even if virtual transactions were to match 

the quantity of under-scheduled renewables, the prices associated with the virtual offers are not 

likely to fully represent the offer prices of the renewable resources to preserve profit margins.    

In general, virtual transactions were profitable in the SPP market.  Gross profit before fees was 

$186 million in 2023, down from $369 million in 2022.  When charges and transaction fees are 

included, the net profit for virtual transactions was $75 million in 2023, down from $116 million 

in 2022. 

Generation offered in the day-ahead market averaged just over 65 percent and market 

commitment status followed by self-commitment status at 13 percent of the total capacity 

commitments for 2023.  This is a continuation in the trend, which started in 2016, of increasing 

market commitments and decreasing self-commitments. 

SPP designed a ramp capability product, which was implemented on March 1, 2022.2  While the 

MMU generally supported the proposed design, the MMU did have some concerns with the 
design prior to its implementation.3  In addition to these concerns, the MMU identified an issue, 

after implementation, with the deliverability of ramp cleared by the ramp-up capability product.  

2 Tariff Revisions to Add Ramp Capability, FERC Docket No. ER20-1617. 
3 See MMU comments in Docket No. ER20-1617. 

State of the Market 2023 

6 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

The MMU’s pre-implementation concerns and deliverability concerns are both discussed in 

Section 3.2.4. 

SPP implemented an uncertainty product on July 6, 2023.  The product’s objective is similar to 

the ramp product in that it was designed to reorganize the market dispatch, to efficiently ensure 

rampable capacity for future needs, while effectively pricing and paying for the procured 

rampable capacity.  There are two main differences between the uncertianty product and the 

ramp product.  First, the ramp product has a 10 minute assessment horizon while the 

uncertainty product has a one hour horizon, for which they can both procure ramp.  Second, the 

uncertainty product allows clearing from offline and online resources, while the ramp product 

only clears from online resources.  There are several key issues with the current design of the 

uncertainty product, which are discussed in Section 3.2.5. 

1.3  TRANSMISSION CONGESTION AND HEDGING 

Locational marginal prices reflect the sum of the marginal cost of energy, the marginal cost of 

congestion, and the marginal cost of losses for each pricing interval at any given pricing location 

in the market.  Certain locations of the footprint experience significant price movements 

resulting from congestion caused by high wind generation and transmission limitations. 

The areas that experienced the highest congestion costs in 2023 were the northwest corner of 

North Dakota, southeastern Oklahoma, and a concentrated area in southeast North Dakota near 

the MISO seam.  Much of Kansas, Nebraska, South Dakota, and northwest Oklahoma 

experienced the lowest congestion costs for the year. 

The 2023 frequently constrained area study identified the Joplin, Missouri; Tulsa, Oklahoma; 

Oklahoma City, Oklahoma; and Williston, North Dakota areas as these areas continued to see 

elevated congestion prices throughout 2023. 

Total congestion payments for 2023 were nearly $1.4 billion; down from the $2 billion in 2022 

but up from $1.2 billion in 2021.  The decrease seen in 2023 can be mostly attributed to 

reductions in fuel supply costs. 

While most load-serving entities were able to successfully hedge their congestion exposure with 

auction revenue rights and transmission congestion rights, a handful of participants were under-

hedged.  The largest amount over-hedged was $127 million, while the largest amount under-

hedged was $43 million. 

State of the Market 2023 

7 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

1.4  UPLIFT COSTS 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

Generators receive make-whole payments to ensure that they receive sufficient revenue to cover 

energy, start-up, no-load, and operating reserve costs for both market and local reliability 

commitments.  A make-whole payment is an additional market payment that supplements a 

resource’s revenue if prices fall below its cleared offer.  These payments are intended to make 

resources whole to energy, commitment, and operating reserve costs. 

For 2023, combined day-ahead and reliability unit commitment make-whole payments totaled 

$236 million.  Reliability unit commitment make-whole payments represented 66 percent of the 

total, with day-ahead make-whole payments accounting for the other 34 percent.   

Day-ahead make-whole payments were nearly $79 million in 2023, down from $172 million in 

2022; a decrease of 54 percent.  Reliability unit commitment make-whole payments in 2023 

totaled $157 million, down from $292 million in 2022; representing a nearly a 47 percent 

decrease.  The decline in the level of make-whole payments from 2022 to 2023 can be mostly 

attributed to lower gas prices during the year. 

Revenue neutrality uplift (RNU) ensures settlement payments and receipts for each real-time 

hourly settlement interval equals zero.  Positive revenue neutrality uplift indicates that SPP 

received insufficient revenue, initiating additional collections from market participants.  

Inversely, negative revenue-neutrality uplift indicates that SPP received excess revenue, which 

must then be credited back to market participants. 

Total monthly revenue neutrality uplift for 2023 was $205 million, down over 62 percent from 

$546 million in 2022.  The two main components of revenue neutrality uplift are real-time 

congestion and the real-time joint operating agreement (also known as market-to-market).  The 

joint operating agreement component generally acts to lessen revenue neutrality uplift, while 

the congestion component generally acts to increase revenue neutrality uplift.  The real-time 

joint operating agreement portion of revenue neutrality uplift was $94 million in 2023, a 

decrease of 43 percent; from $165 million in 2022.  The real-time congestion component of 

revenue neutrality uplift was $283 million in 2023, a decrease of nearly 57 percent from $654 

million in 2022.   

1.5  COMPETITIVENESS ASSESSMENT 

Overall, structural and behavioral metrics indicate that the SPP markets have been competitive 

over the last several years.  The market share, Herfindahl–Hirschman Index (HHI), and pivotal 

State of the Market 2023 

8 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

supplier analysis indicate minimal to moderate potential structural market power in SPP markets, 

outside of frequently congested areas.   

The market share indicator in 2023 continued a trend of decreasing concentration after an acute 

rise following an intra-market merger that formed Evergy, Inc. in June 2018.  Market shares by 

the largest supplier exceeded the 20 percent threshold in 29 percent of hours during 2023, 

down from 45 percent in 2022.   

Meanwhile, another general measure of structural market power—the Herfindahl-Hirschman 

Index (HHI) calculation for supplier concentration—pointed to an incrementally elevated 

concentration year-over-year and a higher concentration generally, following the 

aforementioned merger.  The HHI market concentration analysis showed that three percent of 

hours were considered moderately concentrated in 2023, a decrease from 2022.  The decrease in 

both the market share indicator and HHI indicate decreasing structural market power in the SPP 

market. 

Structural market power in the SPP footprint only creates the potential for market manipulation.  

The MMU maintains its belief that the existing local market power mitigation measures are 

sufficiently robust to moderate the impact of an actual exercise of that potential, should it occur.  

The MMU will continue to evaluate structural market power concerns going forward.   

Any exercise of market power is most likely to be profitable in transmission-constrained areas 

where concerns regarding potential local market power are highest.  MMU analysis and 

continued scrutiny of potential areas under frequent constraints confirm that existing mitigation 

measures are effective to mitigate the exercise of local market power.  

Behavioral indicators were also assessed by analyzing the conduct of market participants and 

the impact of that conduct on market prices, to detect the exercise of market power.  The MMU 

examined offer price markups, offer quantities, mitigation frequency, and measures of implied 

economic and physical withholding by evaluating market behavior.  The MMU noted overall 

improvements in the convergence of market price and imputed costs, but still observes negative 

prices at a level and frequency that warrants continued concern.  In particular, wind units had 

exceptionally low markups – a concern that the MMU has raised in prior Annual State of the 

Market reports. 

Positive markups were observed for coal at slightly elevated levels relative to the positive 

markups seen in 2021, but much lower than 2022.  The coal markup levels were $6.88/MWh in 

2023, $21.12/MWh in 2022, and $6.02/MWh in 2021 respectively.  While negative on-peak 

State of the Market 2023 

9 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

markups were observed in almost every month for gas in 2023, with a clear correlation to the 

prevailing price of gas within the SPP footprint.   

Nonetheless, all months saw negative markups for wind resources, with markup levels 

continuing to deeply decrease throughout the year, to levels generally lower than those seen in 

2022.  The average markups for wind resources were -$41.50/MWh in 2023, -$38.75/MWh in 

2022, and -$29.03/MWh in 2021 respectively.  

Compared with the previous year, the output gap for 2023 was slightly lower.  Overall, the 

output gap remains at very low levels, averaging less than 0.2 percent in all months; reflecting a 

high level of participation in the market overall.   

Mitigation for economic withholding remains relatively infrequent overall, with a small increase 

in day-ahead energy offer mitigation.  Mitigation of no-load offers remain low as well, while 

operating reserve mitigation increased over 2022. This was largely concentrated in spinning 

reserve resource intervals during the spring and fall. 

The remaining metrics suggest that actual withholding behavior is infrequent.  The measured 

economic output gap was slightly lower in 2023 compared to 2022 and the amount of physically 

unoffered generation remained at normal levels, indicating reasonable levels of total unoffered 

economic capacity in 2023. 

Overall, the SPP Integrated Marketplace provides effective market incentives and mitigation 

measures to produce competitive market outcomes, particularly during market intervals where 

the exercise of local market power is a concern.  While some behavioral indicators increased 

relative to low prior-year levels, these largely reflected conditions external to the market, and 

structural indicators showed continued levels of diverse and healthy competition.  Market 

behaviors and results remained workably competitive overall, only infrequently requiring 

mitigation of local market power to achieve those outcomes.  Nonetheless, mitigation of 

economic withholding remains an essential tool in ensuring that market results are competitive 

during periods when such market conditions offer suppliers the potential to abuse local market 

power. 

1.6  RESOURCE ADEQUACY ISSUES 

The winter weather events of February 2021, December 2022, and January 2022 highlighted 

significant issues with SPP’s resource adequacy approach.  The MMU and SPP outlined several of 

State of the Market 2023 

10 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

these issues in our winter weather reports.4  Outside of winter, resource advisories and 

conservative operations have been declared during periods of mild weather when a combination 

of outages and lower wind output have led to tight conditions.  Key issues with the current 

supply adequacy approach include a lack of a seasonal resource adequacy requirement, fuel 

availability risk, correlated output and outages among similar resources, and an accreditation 

process that does not reflect actual resource performance.  

SPP currently has a resource adequacy requirement for summer.  Stakeholders submitted tariff 

revisions to FERC to establish a winter resource adequacy requirement in September 2023.  

However, the filing was rejected on the grounds that the filing did not require a reasonable 

expectation of availability.  SPP and stakeholders are in the process of revising the requirement 

with updated availability language.  While this is a necessary first step, capacity can also be tight 

in the shoulder periods as resources take maintenance outages, something the MMU observed 

in the last few years.  Resource adequacy is something that needs to be considered year-round, 

not just in the summer. 

Resources experienced significant fuel supply issues that limited their availability.  While almost 

all fuel-types experienced some issue during the winter weather events, by far, both natural gas 

and coal resources in the SPP region experienced a high level of outages related to fuel supply 

limitations.  Though SPP may not experience adverse weather events every year, correlations in 

forced outages due to fuel supply shocks need to be incorporated into any resource adequacy 

approach to ensure a more accurate accounting of the expected availability of resources.  

Correlated forced outages were not included in the stakeholder approved performance-based 
accreditation methodology filed with FERC in February 2024.5 

SPP does not have an effective mechanism to measure and incorporate performance in resource 

availability accreditation.  The current accreditation methodology for conventional resources 

does not consider resource performance beyond an operational test.  While SPP and 

stakeholders have submitted a new effective load carrying capability (ELCC) accreditation 

methodology for variable energy resources and a performance-based accreditation (PBA) 

methodology for conventional resources.  While both of these methodologies consider historical 

performance, the latter does not consider out of management control forced outages, 

maintenance, or planned outages.  As such, a gap between accreditation and expected 

4 SPP MMU Report on February 2021 Winter Weather Event, SPP's Response to the February 2021 Winter 
Weather Event, Summary, A Comprehensive Review of SPP's Response to the February 2021 Winter Storm 
report 
5 See ER24-1317 

State of the Market 2023 

11 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

availability will likely remain if the proposed accreditation processes go into effect without 

further changes, including weighting of conventional resource performance during critical 

periods. 

Additionally, the MMU does not believe the subset of outages included, combined with 

averaging performance over a seven year period, will fully incentivize resources to address 

shortfalls in their performance.  Moreover, it will not reward resources that have taken steps to 

improve their performance, removing the incentive for LREs to make substantial investments to 

improve availability.  As such, the MMU continues to recommend that SPP and stakeholders 

develop availability incentives to enhance resource performance and reliability. 

Addressing resource adequacy is perhaps the most important lesson from the winter storms of 

February 2021, December 2022, and January 2024.  The SPP system was lucky to have significant 

imports from MISO, PJM, and others; particularly in 2021 and 2024.  SPP should not depend on 

these systems in future planning models, as a wider regional cold snap could limit imports.  In 

December 2022 and January 2024, SPP avoided severe outcomes largely because of the 

availability of wind – another condition SPP should not always rely on.  Effectively improving 

resource adequacy will require seasonal variation, effective measurement of performance, and a 

requirement that counts for different contingencies and incentives.  The MMU has engaged and 

will continue to engage in the SPP stakeholder processes, to help promote improved resource 

adequacy. 

1.7  RECOMMENDATIONS 

One of the primary responsibilities of a market monitoring unit is evaluating market rules and 

design features, to improve market efficiency and effectiveness.  When issues are identified 

within the market, one of the ways to correct them is to make recommendations on market 

enhancements.  These recommendations are documented in more detail in Chapter 7.  The 

MMU is making two new recommendations for 2023, as well as highlighting some existing 

recommendations, in an effort to promote the need for these issues to be addressed. 

  NEW RECOMMENDATIONS 

2023.1  Enhance uncertainty product design to ensure procurement of adequate rampable 

capacity 

The MMU observed that the uncertainty product’s day-ahead prices were set at zero dollars for 

99 percent of the hours in the day-ahead market and every interval, except one, in the real-time 

market.  One reason for these low market clearing prices is that the product’s requirement was 

State of the Market 2023 

12 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

set at zero megawatts during 82 percent of hours in 2023.  This was due to two factors:  backing 

out of other reserve product requirements from the uncertainty product’s requirement and the 

treatment of soon-to-start resources in the optimization.   

The MMU recommends that SPP review the requirement settings for the uncertainty product 

and make corrections to ensure adequate rampable capacity is procured through the product.  

In particular, the MMU recommends that SPP look at the practice of reducing the uncertainty 

requirement with other reserve products’ requirements. 

In addition, soon-to-start resources are considered to have a zero-dollar opportunity cost and 

are not compensated for the rampable uncertainty they provide.  This means that soon-to-start 

resources effectively further reduce the requirement in the 18 percent of hours the requirement 

was non-zero.  The MMU recommends SPP review the use of soon-to-start resources in meeting 

the requirement for uncertainty.  Soon-to-start resources are not counted in any other way 

when optimizing other market products such as energy or ancillary services. 

The MMU included analysis of the uncertainty product in its Fall 2023 Quarterly State of the 
Market report detailing these recommendations,6 and these are discussed in more detail in 

Section 3.2.5.  These recommendations have been submitted for consideration as part of the 
SPP Roadmap7 ranking process.  

2023.2 Ensure planning, operations, and markets processes appropriately consider 

integration of large loads 

The number of large loads, such as large data centers and crypto mines, continue to rise in the 
SPP footprint, with many of them registering as demand response resources.8  Owing to their 

size and location, these loads have the potential to cause, and have caused high prices and 

significant levels of congestion in both the day-ahead and real-time markets.  Alignment of 

planning, markets, and operational processes to accommodate the integration of these large 

loads is necessary to ensure appropriate price signals in the market and address congestion 

impacts on the system.  Furthermore, alignment also helps prevent operational issues that 

ultimately impact the market and other market participants.   

6 Fall 2023 Quarterly State of the Market report 
7 The SPP Roadmap is a process where SPP staff and stakeholders identify, educate, rank, and approve new 
and existing Integrated Marketplace initiatives for development over the next two to five years.  More 
information on this process can be found at https://www.spp.org/stakeholder-center/spp-roadmap/. 
https://www.spp.org/stakeholder-center/spp-roadmap/ 
8 Summer 2023 Quarterly State of the Market report

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

For planning processes, the MMU recommends implementing procedures that help ensure that 

any generation needed to meet new loads can be reasonably expected to be available, if 

needed.  In some cases, generation to meet this load may not be available on a continual basis 

due to known regulatory, environmental, or other operational reasons, and therefore such 

contingent circumstances should be incorporated into SPP’s planning processes.   

With regard to the market, the MMU recommends enhancements that include, but are not 

limited to:  1) tariff enhancements that provide for a more specific mitigated offer methodology 

design, to ensure the cost of demand response loads are appropriately considered in the 

market; 2) enhancements to the registration process for those loads registering as demand 

response and ensuring appropriate registration requirements are met; 3) ensure the cost of 

congestion and out-of-market actions are appropriately aligned with the load causing the 

issues; and 4) identifying solutions that allow for appropriate price signals in the market.   

The MMU recognizes that operational decisions may also impact the market.  The MMU 

recommends additional transparency into out-of-market actions taken to address congestion 

and reliability issues, caused by the integration of these loads.  This includes operational 

procedures that may prevent appropriate pricing signals in the market and ensuring consistency 

as new loads are integrated.

of the ramping capability product demand curve.

2018.1  Limit the exercise of market power by creating a backstop for parameter changes 

In the 2018 Annual State of the Market report, the MMU recommended that SPP strengthen the 

language regarding non-dollar-based parameters so that the expectation for the basis of these 

values is clear, well-defined, and based on the actual capabilities of the resource.  This 
recommendation was added as an initiative 17 on the SPP Roadmap in 2020 as a high priority.  

As of the timing of this report, work has not started on this initiative.   

This MMU is highlighting the need for this recommendation to be addressed due to continued 

concerns regarding non-dollar-based parameters being used to manipulate market results and 

prices.   As noted in the 2018 recommendation, the market is vulnerable to the exercise of 

market power by the manipulation of resources’ non-dollar-based parameters and that non-

dollar-based parameters should not be manipulated to affect market clearing.   

The MMU continues to recommend tariff changes be made to address potential impacts to 

market results and prices through the manipulation of non-dollar-based resource parameters.  

The MMU recommends SPP include language in its tariff that requires information such as 

registration data, resource offers, and outage information to reasonably reflect the actual 

capabilities of the resources in the market. 

  WINTER WEATHER EVENT – CRITICAL RECOMMENDATIONS 

The recommendations below represent three critical areas the MMU emphasized in its written 
report on the February 2021 winter weather event.18 

WWE1  Ensure availability of resources 

An accurate measure of resource-level availability, on a seasonal basis to meet seasonal 

demands, remains on the MMU’s list of critical recommendations for reliability.  An inadequate 

estimate of capacity that will be available during peak demand or a system shock, such as a 

weather event, leads to the reliance on neighboring regions and unaccredited capacity to keep 

the lights on.  If the scope of the system shock is large enough, as it was in February 2021, it will 

inevitably lead to a load shedding event.   

17 SIR 22 - Limit Market Power Through Physical Parameters 
18 SPP MMU Winter Weather Report 2021 

State of the Market 2023 

16 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

In July 2022, the SPP Board of Directors approved the policy paper, recommended by SPP and 

approved by the Market and Operations Policy Committee (MOPC) and the Supply Adequacy 

Working Group (SAWG), for performance based accreditation (PBA) for conventional resources 

using a variant of a demand-weighted probabilistic equivalent forced outage rate demand 
(EFORd).19  SPP filed this methodology, along with a modified effective load carrying capability 
(ELCC) methodology for variable energy resources, with FERC in late February 2024.20  The MMU 
subsequently filed its comments in late March.21  Using performance based accreditation and 

effective load carrying capability for resource accreditation, is an improvement over the status 

quo and is a first step in improving resource accreditation.  However, follow-up items remain.  

For instance, one follow-up item relates to the weighting of performance based accreditation 

accredited resources during the highest value hours.  Another follow-up item is regarding the 

treatment of out of management control outages for variable energy resources using effective 

load carrying capability.   

To improve resource adequacy, and to ensure non-discriminatory treatment of resources, the 

MMU recommends that SPP and its stakeholders continue to work on these important and 

necessary follow-up initiatives.   

WWE2 Establish incentive mechanism for accredited capacity 

The MMU continues to see the need for implementing both market and non-market 

mechanisms to incentivize resource attributes that enhance reliability through resiliency and 

availability.  The MMU recommends the development of market and non-market incentives for 

“reliability attributes”, in conjunction with the development of an accurate measurement for 

resource availability as two legs of a three-legged stool that ensures reliability through 

measurements, incentives, and requirements.  The MMU proposed a set of market mechanisms 

to incentivize existing resources to increase their availability to the market and efficiently 

schedule maintenance outages, through valuing the availability of capacity when it is needed in 

the region.  By placing a value of availability, participants can make economic choices on which 

resource attributes can be improved to most economically increase availability based on 

resource type, geographic location, and environmental and governmental policies.   

19 SPP Board of Directors action: 
https://www.spp.org/documents/67635/bod_mc%20minutes%202022%2007%2026.pdf 
20 FERC Docket ER24-1317 
21 MMU Comments on ER24-1317.pdf 

State of the Market 2023 

17 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 1 
Executive summary 

The MMU initiative was ranked as “high” in the 2023 SPP Roadmap prioritization.  However, little 

progress has been made and stakeholders have expressed a desire to wait for results from the 
proposed performance credit mechanism, pursued in the ERCOT market.22   

The MMU included recommendations in its winter weather report, to allow for meaningful 

incentives for availability noting that, to the extent that a resource is more available, there 

should be incentives.  Additionally, to the extent that a resource is less available, there should be 

disincentives.  The MMU continues to recommend the establishment of an incentive mechanism 

for actual resource performance for accredited capacity.  As mentioned above, despite 

improvements made by the proposed performance based accreditation methodology for 

conventional resources and effective load carrying capability methodology for variable energy 

resources, the MMU does not anticipate this new accreditation approach alone will sufficiently 

incentivize resources to be more available. 

WWE3 Establish more frequent resource adequacy requirement 

The MMU continues to emphasize the uniqueness of seasonal demands and the resource 

attributes needed to meet those demands.  The MMU recommends that SPP resource adequacy 

requirements address resource needs to meet the demand in each season independently.  While 

the region tends to experience the greatest demand and system shocks during summer and 

winter, availability tends to be lowest in the spring and fall seasons, which have no obligation or 

requirement.   

SPP is currently working with stakeholders to change the winter season resource obligation into 

a requirement, complete with penalty charges for deficiency.  SPP and stakeholders filed an 

initial tariff revision establishing a winter resource adequacy requirement, in September 2023.  

However, the filing was rejected by FERC on the grounds that there was not a reasonable 

expectation of availability for those resources accredited to meet the requirement.  A new filing 

is expected in the coming months.  

The MMU is engaged in the stakeholder process in support of adding a winter requirement, but 

additionally recommends the same effort be undertaken for the spring and fall seasons.  

Moreover, to ensure resources are still able to complete necessary maintenance, the MMU 

recommends coupling the outage coordination process with the supply adequacy process to 

ensure regional availability needs and individual maintenance needs can be balanced without 

22 The Performance Credit Mechanism has elements similar to the MMU’s proposed availability incentive 
approach.

impacting reliability.  The MMU sees the seasonal resource adequacy requirement as the final 

leg of a balanced approach, which uses accurate measurements, meaningful incentives, and 

adequate requirements to ensure reliability. 

  PREVIOUS RECOMMENDATIONS 

Discussion of other previous recommendations can be found in Chapter 7. 

IMPLEMENTED RECOMMENDATIONS 

The following recommendations from previous reports were implemented in 2023. 

2018.3  Develop compensation mechanism to pay for capacity to cover uncertainties 

In the 2018 report, the MMU recommended SPP develop a compensation mechanism to pay for 

capacity needed to address uncertainties in the market.  SPP’s market participants approved a 
revision request23 to implement an uncertainty product in April 2021.  The Tariff changes for the 

uncertainty product design were approved by FERC in August 2022.  Implementation of the 
uncertainty product was completed July 6, 2023.24  The MMU considers this recommendation 

closed.  

2014.3  Address gaming opportunity for multi-day minimum run time resources 

The MMU recommended changes to address a gaming opportunity in the market for resources 

with minimum run times greater than two days in its 2014 report.  While tariff changes to 

address this concern were approved by the SPP board in 2018, subsequent changes were 

needed to address inconsistent tariff language that the revisions revealed but did not address.  
An associated revision request25 and additional tariff modification was approved through the 
stakeholder process.  SPP filed these changes with FERC on May 7, 202026 and the MMU filed 
comments in support of the tariff changes on June 12, 2020.27  These changes were 

implemented on October 1, 2023.  The MMU considers this recommendation closed.

2  LOAD AND RESOURCES 

This chapter reviews load and resources in the SPP market for 2023.  Key points from this 

chapter include: 

•  Monthly average system energy consumption was down one-half percent compared to 

2022 levels.  Most of this decrease can be attributed to lessened weather impacts. 

• 

In 2023, 1,700 MW of wind generation capacity was added to the market.  Wind 

generation capacity now accounts for 34 percent of installed nameplate capacity in the 

SPP market. 

•  As of December 31, 2023, there were 132 demand response resources in the SPP market, 

representing 793 MW of nameplate capacity.  This represents a net loss of eight 

resources compared to the end of 2022, but a net gain of 431 MW of nameplate 

capacity.  During the year, 64 dispatchable demand response resources retired, 

representing 25 MW of nameplate capacity, and 56 dispatchable demand response 

resources were added to the market, representing 449 MW of nameplate capacity. 

•  The generation interconnection queue has 135,000 MW of requested projects at the end 

of 2023.  Of the queue, about 40,000 MW are wind resources, 46,000 MW are solar 

resources, 25,000 MW are battery/storage resources, with the remainder hybrid or gas 

simple-cycle resources. 

•  Wind generation represented the largest portion of total energy produced at 37 percent 

of the total.  Coal generation was next highest at 27 percent of the total.   

•  SPP remained a net exporter for 2023 with an hourly average of 418 MW, down from 601 

MW in 2022.   

•  Net market-to-market payments from MISO to SPP were $93 million, 42 percent lower 

than 2022 payments of $160 million.  

•  Cleared virtual energy bids and offers as a percentage of load for 2023 was 26 percent, 

down from 29 percent in 2022. 

•  Average profit per cleared virtual megawatt after fees was $1.10/MW, down 25 percent 

from 2022.

2.1  THE INTEGRATED MARKETPLACE 

SPP is a Regional Transmission Organization (RTO) authorized by the Federal Energy Regulatory 

Commission (FERC) to ensure reliable power supplies, adequate transmission infrastructure, and 

competitive wholesale electricity prices.  FERC granted RTO status to SPP in 2004.  SPP provides 

many services to its members, including reliability coordination, tariff administration, regional 

scheduling, reserve sharing, transmission expansion planning, wholesale electricity market 

operations, and training.  This report focuses on the 2023 calendar year of the SPP wholesale 

electricity market referred to as the Integrated Marketplace, which started on March 1, 2014. 

The Integrated Marketplace has a full day-ahead market with transmission congestion rights, 

virtual trading, a reliability unit commitment process, a real-time balancing market, and a price-

based operating reserves market.  SPP simultaneously put into operation a single balancing 

authority as part of the implementation of the Integrated Marketplace.  The primary benefit of a 

day-ahead market is improved efficiency of daily resource commitments.  Another benefit of this 

market includes the joint optimization of the available capacity for energy and operating 

reserves. 

SPP MARKET FOOTPRINT 

The SPP market footprint is located in the westernmost portion of the Eastern Interconnection, 

with Midcontinent ISO (MISO) to the east, Electric Reliability Council of Texas (ERCOT) to the 

south, and the Western Interconnection to the west.  Figure 2–1 shows the current operating 

regions of the nine RTO/ISO markets in the United States and Canada, as well as a more detailed 

view of the SPP footprint.  The SPP market also has connections with other non-RTO/ISO areas 

such as Saskatchewan Power Corporation, Associated Electric Cooperative, and Southwestern 
Power Administration.28  

28 Southwestern Power Administration belongs to the SPP RTO, Reliability Coordinator (RC), and Reserve 
Sharing Group (RSG) footprints.  Associated Electric Cooperative belongs to the SPP RSG.

electric cooperatives, municipal utilities, federal and state agencies, independent power 

producers, and financial-only market participants that do not own physical assets.  Figure 2–2

2.2  ELECTRICITY DEMAND  

  SYSTEM PEAK DEMAND 

Chapter 2 
Load and resources 

One way to evaluate load is to review peak system demand statistics over an extended period.  

The market footprint has changed over time as participants have been added to or withdrawn 

from the market.  The peak demand values reviewed in this section are coincident peaks, 

calculated out of total generation dispatch across the entire market footprint that occurred 

during a specific real-time market five-minute interval.  The peak experienced during a particular 

year or season is affected by events such as unusually hot or cold weather, daily and seasonal 

load patterns, and economic growth and change. 

Figure 2–4 shows a month-by-month comparison of five-minute demand for the last three 

years.

In 2023, the maximum hourly average load was 53,555 MW, which was up five percent from

Comparing annual load duration curves shows differentiation between cases of extreme loading 

events and more general increases in system demand.  If only the extremes are higher or lower 

than the previous year, then short-term loading events are likely the reason.  If the entire curve 

is higher across the entire range, this is more indicative of increased system demand.  For 2023, 

the curve remains slightly below 2022 from around the fifth to the fiftieth percentile, indicating a 

slight decrease in system demand.  However, both ends of the curve were higher in 2023 than in 

2022, indicating increased short-term loading events.

As shown in the chart, cooling degree days are more prevalent in the higher load months of 

May through September, whereas heating degree days are more prevalent in the other months.  

Total degree day impact in 2023 was below 2022, but near 2021 levels.  Average hourly load had 

a 5.6 percent increase from 2021 to 2022 but dropped by 0.8 percent from 2022 to 2023. 

Figure 2–10, Figure 2–11, and Figure 2–12 show load levels, cooling degree days, and heating 
degree days for the past three years compared to a normal year.30  Normal load was derived 

29 To determine heating degree days and cooling degree days for the SPP footprint, several representative 
locations are used in the calculation.  These locations include Shreveport LA, Lubbock TX, Oklahoma City 
OK, Amarillo TX, Kansas City, MO, Hays, KS, Omaha NE, North Platte NE, Sioux Falls SD, Rapid City SD, 
Grand Forks ND, and Williston/Stanley ND.  The base temperature separating heating and cooling periods 
is 65 degrees Fahrenheit.  If the average temperature of a day at a location is 75 degrees Fahrenheit, there 
would be 10 (=75–65) cooling degree days at that location.  If a day’s average temperature is 50 degrees 
Fahrenheit, there would be 15 (=65–50) heating degree days at that location.  Using statistical tools, the 
daily estimated load impact of a single cooling degree day is just over four times higher than the impact of 
a single heating degree day.  This is in part because more electric is used for cooling than electric heating.  
So, in order to show the actual impact of degree days, cooling degree days are multiplied by 4.2 in Figure 
2–9. 
30 The 30-year normal temperatures are from the 1991-2020 U.S. Climate Normals product from the 
National Oceanic and Atmospheric Association (NOAA).

 Total installed nameplate generation capacity in the SPP Integrated Marketplace was 100,440 
MW at the end of 2023, representing an increase of 1.8 percent (or 1,806 MW) from 2022.31  This 

increase was driven by a five percent increase (1,693 MW) in nameplate wind capacity in 2023.  

The total 2023 market share of wind capacity represented 34 percent of total nameplate 

generation. 

When both types of natural gas resources are combined, natural gas-fired installed generation 

capacity still represents the largest share of generation capacity in the SPP market at 36 percent 

(gas simple-cycle 23 percent and gas combined-cycle 14 percent) of nameplate capacity, with 

coal being the third largest type at 23 percent.  Also of note, an additional 431 MW of 

nameplate capacity of dispatchable demand response resources was added to the market in 

2023, bringing the total nameplate capacity of these resources to 793 MW. 

Although not yet a significant portion of nameplate capacity, SPP has three registered market 

storage resources, representing 12 MW of capacity.  While currently only a small portion of total

As of the end of 2023, 42 percent of SPP’s generation fleet is more than 30 years old.  In 

particular, 86 percent of coal capacity and 42 percent of gas capacity is older than 30 years.  

According to the U.S. Energy Information Administration (EIA), the national average retirement 
age of coal-fired generation in 2023 was 53 years.32  Aside from the resources that joined SPP 
from Nebraska in 2009 and the Integrated System33 in 2015, the largest source of new capacity 

in the SPP footprint over the last 10 years has been wind capacity.

Almost all the coal and gas capacity retired since 2016 has been 1950s era plants.  Most of the 

wind units that retired in recent years were first-generation wind resources.  Of the 738 MW of 

retired capacity in 2023, 677 MW came from coal, 36 from gas, and 25 MW of dispatchable 

demand response resources. 

Total nameplate capacity additions were 2,641 MW in 2023.  For capacity additions, wind 

generation has accounted for 85 percent of the additions over the last three years.  Of note, only 

1,705 MW of wind generation was added in 2023, this is down markedly from 4,779 MW in 

2020, 3,792 MW in 2021, but slightly higher than 1,579 MW added in 2022.  Even with the 

increased amount of solar generation in the generation interconnection queue, only 235 MW of 

solar resources were added in 2023.  This is still indicative of a challenging interconnection 

process.  Considering the 2,641 MW in capacity additions along with 738 MW of retirements, 
1,903 MW34 of net generating capacity was added to the SPP market in 2023.

Total aggregate real-time generation supply for summer 2023 was 78,157 MW, compared to 

76,905 MW for summer 2022, an increase of 1.6 percent.  The system peak demand in 2023 of 

56,138 MW was about five percent higher than 2022.  However, the average demand had a 1.6 

percent decrease from 2022 to 2023.  Based on the heating and cooling degree days analysis in 

Section 2.2.4, the SPP market footprint had much lower cooling degree days in July 2023 

compared to 2022, which resulted in lower average demand in the summer months as 

compared to the previous year. 

Also evident is the approximately 22.4 GW gap between this maximum supply and the total 

installed nameplate generation capacity on the peak summer day.  This is primarily a result of 

the difference between the wind forecast and installed capacity of wind resources 

(approximately 17 GW), resources reporting on outage (approximately 5 GW), and reduced 

summer capacity due to high ambient temperatures (less than 0.4 GW).

The total aggregate real-time generation supply for winter 2023 was 70,044 MW, compared to 

64,305 MW for winter 2022, an eight percent increase.  The system winter peak demand of 

40,807 MW for 2023 was 15 percent lower than 2022.  The high peak demand in 2022 was 

attributed to the December 2022 winter weather event.  Although the winter peak demand 

decreased by nearly 15 percent, the winter average demand increased by about two percent 

from 2022 to 2023. 

The section of the offer curve below $0/MWh is mostly due to wind and solar energy and can 

vary between 1,000 MW and 20,000 MW, based on wind and solar availability.  Negative offers 

typically reflect opportunity costs associated with state and federal tax incentives.  The sharp 

uptick in price at the top of the supply curves of 2022 and 2023 represents the transition from 

natural gas units to oil units.  Due to the significantly high gas price during the winter event in 

February 2021, the supply curve of 2021 does not reach the sharp uptick stage under the 

$1,600/MWh price as several offers were at or above the $2,000/MWh offer cap.  

Comparing the supply curves of 2022 and 2023, prices shift at around 40 GW for both the  

summer and winter curves in 2022, which likely represents the transition from coal units to 

natural gas units at high natural gas prices.

As shown above, generation capacity from renewable, storage, and hybrid36 resources accounts

As the chart above shows, at the end of 2023, generation totaling just over 23 GW has an 

executed generation request that is on-schedule to be added to the market in 2024 and beyond, 

with wind representing 15 GW of this generation.  It is important to note that of the on-schedule 

projects, 66 percent are for wind resources, 25 percent are for solar resources, and nine percent 

are for battery/storage resources.  However, when looking at the entire generation 

interconnection queue, 35 percent of the projects are for solar resources, 30 percent are for 

wind resources, 19 percent are for battery/storage resources, and 12 percent are for hybrid 

resources.  Although, solar resources make up a larger portion of the entire queue, the fact that 

wind resources represent a larger portion of the on-schedule projects in the queue indicates 

that wind resources will still be the predominant type of resource added to the market in the 

near future.  Also key, is that generation can still be added or removed from the on-schedule 

project list, even in the current year.  However, there is more surety to the levels of generation 

scheduled to go into production closer to the current year, and additions and deletions to on-

schedule projects are more typical in future years.

Some of the annual fluctuations in generation by technology type shares are driven by the 

relative difference in primary fuel prices, namely natural gas versus coal.  The long-term trend 

for coal-fired generation had been relatively flat prior to 2014 (not shown on chart above) but 

has been in a steady decline ever since.  This is primarily due to low gas prices from 2015 to 

2020, causing efficient gas generation to displace some goal generation, as can be seen in the 

higher generation from combined-cycle gas plants.  Higher gas prices in 2021 and 2022 led to a 

temporary reversal of this trend.  Coal generation became more economic than many gas 

resources, resulting in an increase in the percentage of total generation by coal to 36 percent 

year over year.  However, after gas prices reverted to previous levels, coal dropped to 33 percent 

of total generation in 2022, and then dropped further to 27 percent of total generation in 2023. 

After steadily climbing, wind generation share dropped from 37.4 percent in 2022 to 36.6 

percent in 2023.  The drop in wind generation can primarily be attributed to lower average wind 

speeds in 2023, as shown in Figure 2–27.  After higher gas prices in 2021 and 2022, generation 

from gas units such as gas turbines and gas steam turbines increased back near 2020 levels, with 

gas, combined-cycle at 17 percent of total generation and gas, simple-cycle at 10 percent.

natural gas technologies are some of the factors that will continue to put pressure on coal 

generation levels.  Renewable generation is expected to continue to increase in the years ahead.

Wind generation as a percentage of total generation is generally lowest in the summer months 

at levels around 25 percent.  The highest wind generation months are during spring and fall. 

During these seasons, monthly wind generation levels can approach, or even exceed, 50 percent 

of total generation.  In 2023, wind reached a high of 54 percent in April.  Additionally, wind 

generation outpaced coal in every month, except for June, July, and August. 

One method commonly used to assess price trends and relative efficiency in electricity markets 

originating from non-fuel costs is the implied heat rate.  The implied heat rate is calculated by 

dividing the electricity price, net of a representative value for variable operations and 
maintenance (VOM) costs, by the fuel (gas) price.37  For a gas generator, the implied heat rate 

serves as a “break-even” point for profitability such that a unit producing output with an 

operating (actual) heat rate below the implied heat rate would be earning profits, given market 

prices for electricity and gas.  If the price of natural gas was $3/MMBtu, and the electricity price 

was $24/MWh, the implied heat rate would be (24/3) = 8 MMBtu/MWh (8,000 Btu/kWh).  This 

37 For the implied heat rate calculation, natural gas units are assumed to be on the margin and accordingly, 
gas prices are taken as the relevant fuel cost.  Emission costs are ignored in fuel cost as they rarely apply in 
the SPP market. 

State of the Market 2023 

41 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 2 
Load and resources 

implied heat rate shows the relative efficiency required of a generator to convert gas to 

electricity and cover the variable costs of production, given market prices. 

Figure 2–22 shows the monthly implied heat rate using real-time electricity prices for 2021 to 

2023, along with an annual average for those years.

As shown above, the implied heat rate for 2023 was significantly above 2021 and 2022, with the 

2021 and 2022 average of about 7,500 Btu/KWh, and the 2023 average of nearly 11,000 

Btu/KWh, an increase of 44 percent.  With implied heat rates in the 15,000 Btu/KWh range 

during the summer months and the actual heat rates for most gas resources below this level, 

this would indicate, at least for the summer months, that most gas resources would be 

profitable given the electricity and gas prices during the summer. 

  GENERATION ON THE MARGIN 

The system marginal price represents the price of the next increment of generation available to 

meet the next increment of total system demand.  The locational marginal price at a particular 

pricing node is the system marginal energy price plus any marginal congestion charges and 

marginal loss charges associated with that pricing node.  For a generator to set the marginal 

price, the resource must be:  1) dispatchable by the market; 2) not at the resource economic 

minimum or maximum; and 3) not ramp limited.  In other words, it must be able to move to 

provide the next increment of generation. 

Day-ahead generation on the margin, shown in Figure 2–23, is different from real-time in that 

the day-ahead market includes virtual transactions.  The real-time market does not include 

State of the Market 2023 

42 

 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 2 
Load and resources 

virtual transactions and is required to adjust to unforeseeable market conditions such as 

unexpected plant and transmission outages.

In 2023, virtual transactions were on the margin the highest percent of hours, at 27 percent in 

the day-ahead market.  Gas, simple-cycle resources accounted for 23 percent of hours, followed 

by coal resources at 21 percent, then wind resources at 17 percent, and lastly, gas, combined-

cycle at 11 percent of hours.  While marginal virtual offers occur at all types of settlement 

locations, 70 percent of marginal virtual offers are at resource settlement locations, with a 

significant amount of that activity at wind generation resource locations.

After peaking at 37 percent in 2022, wind generation on the margin dropped to 30 percent in 

2023.  With the growing amount of dispatchable wind generation and an overall share of 34 

percent of total nameplate capacity, wind generation is increasingly becoming the marginal 

technology a higher percentage of the time.  At the end of 2023, 97 percent of nameplate wind 

capacity was dispatchable, compared to 93 percent at the end of 2021.  At the beginning of the 

Integrated Marketplace in March 2014, just 27 percent of nameplate wind capacity was 

dispatchable.   

The most significant difference between day-ahead and real-time fuel on the margin is the 

absence of virtual offers in the real-time market.  From the day-ahead to the real-time market, 

wind resources on the margin increased from 17 percent to 30 percent of all intervals (13 

percentage points), while gas, simple-cycle and combined-cycle increased by nine and eight 

percentage points, respectively.  Coal resources on the margin dropped by four percentage 

points from the day-ahead to the real-time.  

On a monthly basis, intervals with coal generation on the margin are typically lower in the spring 

and fall months, offset by wind resources acting as base load units.  This results in coal- and gas-

fired units cycling more often.  Increased wind generation also affects prices to some extent 

every month of the year.  The higher wind generation on the margin values in the spring and fall 

are as expected given that these periods are the windiest times of the year, as well as the lowest 

demand periods in the SPP footprint.

in 2023, 64 dispatchable demand response resources retired, representing 25 MW of nameplate 

capacity, with most having a capacity of 0.1 MW.  In 2023, 56 dispatchable demand response

2.6  GROWING IMPACT OF WIND GENERATION CAPACITY 

  WIND CAPACITY AND GENERATION 

The SPP region has a high potential for wind generation given wind patterns in many areas of

additional factors that have resulted in significant investment of wind generation capacity in the

Total registered wind nameplate capacity at the end of 2023 was 33,725 MW, an increase of 

about 1,700 MW from 2022.  At the end of 2023, 97 percent of all nameplate wind capacity was 

dispatchable, while just three percent was non-dispatchable.  Average monthly wind generation 

output decreased by three percent in 2023 to nearly 104,000 GWh.  This three percent decrease 

in average monthly wind generation correlates well to the four percent decrease in wind speed 

year-over-year. 

Consistent with previous years, wind generation fluctuated seasonally with summer being the 

low wind season, while spring and fall were the high wind seasons.  Wind also conformed to 

previous trends where production tended to be lower during on-peak hours than off-peak.  

Finally, higher levels of wind generation tended to coincide with the morning ramp periods.

Wind generation

Wind generation typically produces more energy when demand is lowest and less when demand 

is highest.  Wind generation is at the highest levels in the overnight hours while demand is low.  

In the morning hours, wind generation decreases while load increases.  To navigate these 

transitional hours, available rampable capacity is necessary.  During these transitional hours, 
ramp scarcity is more likely.39  While wind generation is very inexpensive, it must be paired with 

available rampable capacity. 

While Figure 2–33 shows the yearly average load, wind, and net demand, there are seasonal 

differences.  For instance, in the summer, wind generation is lower than during other times of 

the year, and loads are higher.  Thus, the effect on net demand is smaller.  However, in spring 

and fall, loads are lower, and wind can have a significant effect on net demand. 

39 This is discussed in Section 3.2.2.   

State of the Market 2023 

51 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

  WIND INTEGRATION 

Chapter 2 
Load and resources 

Wind integration brings low-cost generation to the SPP region but does not count for much 

accredited capacity.  There are a number of operational challenges in dealing with substantial 

wind capacity.  For instance, wind energy output varies by season and time of day.  This 

variability is estimated to be about four times more than load when measured on an hour-to-

hour basis.  Moreover, wind is counter-cyclical to load.  As load increases (both seasonally and 

daily), wind production typically declines.  The increasing magnitude of wind capacity additions, 

along with the locational concentration, volatility, and timeliness of wind, can create challenges 

for grid operators with regard to managing transmission congestion and resolution of ramping 

constraints as well as challenges for short- and long-run reliability.  Wind forecasting errors can 

lead to price spikes while, the under-clearing of wind in the day-ahead market is a leading cause 

of day-ahead and real-time price divergence.   

In the SPP market, wind and other qualifying resources were allowed to register as non-

dispatchable variable energy resources, provided the resource had an interconnection 

agreement executed by May 21, 2011, and was commercially operated prior to October 15, 

2012.  In April 2019 FERC approved a change of the tariff that required all non-dispatchable 

variable energy resources to become dispatchable by January 1, 2021, or 10 years after 

beginning commercial operations, unless the resource was a run-of-the-river hydro-electric 

facility or exercising its rights under the Public Utility Regulatory Policies Act (PURPA).   

Figure 2–34 illustrates dispatchable variable energy resources (DVERs) and non-dispatchable 

variable energy resources (NDVERs) wind output since 2021.

Of note, non-dispatchable generation continues to trend downward from year to year as more

declined to three percent in 2023. 

Figure 2–35 illustrates average hourly curtailments for wind resources over the past three years.  

In real-time, there are two sources of wind curtailments:  automated market software and 

manual.  Automated market software curtailments occur when wind resources are dispatched 

down by the market system primarily to mitigate transmission constraints, while manual 

curtailments occur when the SPP reliability coordinator issues an out-of-merit-energy (OOME) 

instruction to manage reliability issues that cannot be handled through re-dispatch instructions.

Wind generation

From 2019 to 2022, total average hourly curtailments increased substantially from 137 MW to 

1,260 MW; however, in 2023, total curtailments decreased to 1,097 MW on hourly average basis.  

This occurred primarily because of lower wind generation and additional transmission 

infrastructure.  Manual curtailments as a percentage of total curtailments also continued its 

downward trajectory in 2023, falling to one percent from seven percent in 2022.  This can also 

be attributed to more wind resources becoming dispatchable.  Three historic factors associated 

with the high level of wind curtailments in the SPP market include the large amount of wind 

generation relative to the regional transmission buildout, the counter-cyclical nature of wind 

curtailments compared to load, and the conversion of previously non-dispatchable wind 

resources to dispatchable wind resources.  However, as previously mentioned, two of these 

factors were mitigated in 2023, due to lower wind generation and greater transmission 

availability.

2.7  SEAMS 

  EXPORTS AND IMPORTS 

The SPP Integrated Marketplace has greater than 6,000 MW of AC interties with MISO to the 

east, 720 MW of DC ties to ERCOT to the south, and over 1,000 MW of DC ties to the Western 

Interconnection to the west.  Additionally, SPP has over 1,500 MW of interties with the 

Southwestern Power Administration (SPA) in Arkansas, Missouri, and Oklahoma, and over 5,000 

MW of AC interties with the Associated Electric Cooperative (AECI) in Oklahoma and Missouri.

SPP has been a net exporter in real-time since 2017, prior to that it was a net importer.  

Typically, as wind generation increases, exports increase, though in 2023 net exports were 

highest in the summer high load months.  In 2023, average net exports were highest in 

September with an average of roughly 800 MWh.  Ten of the 12 months averaged positive net 

exports while May and June averaged net imports.  Average net exports for 2023 were 418 

MWh, down from 600 MWh in 2022 and up from 240 MWh in 2021. 

Figure 2–38 through Figure 2–42 show the data for the five most directly connected interfaces in 

real-time, namely ERCOT (includes North and East interfaces), SPA, MISO, AECI, and the Western 

Energy Imbalance Service (WEIS) market.  Tight supply conditions and high prices normally drive 

exports to ERCOT.  While SPP continues to be a net exporter, imports from ERCOT have been 

steadily increasing since 2021, particularly in winter months.  Southwestern Power 

Administration hydropower is imported to serve municipals tied to SPP transmission and is 

highest during on-peak hours but is typically scheduled day-ahead.  MISO interchange generally 

follows wind production, while AECI interchange is coordinated on an ad hoc basis.  Many 

exports and imports with ERCOT and MISO are adjusted based on day-ahead price differences 

in the organized markets and expectations of renewable generation.  Interchange with SPA and 

AECI is less responsive to prices.  The WEIS market has continued to grow its membership since 

its inception in February 2021.  HVDC interfaces with the SPP RTO include Miles City, MT 

(MCWEST); Stegall, NE (SGE); and Sidney, NE (SCSE).  Combined, they have over 500 MW of 

thermal transmission capacity.  In 2023, SPP was a net exporter to WEIS with an annual average 

of nearly 200 MW.  Average exports to WEIS were particularly high in January, February, and 

March, nearly reaching 400 MW.

Market Monitoring Unit 

  MARKET-TO-MARKET 

Chapter 2 
Load and resources 

SPP began the market-to-market (M2M) process with MISO in March 2015 as part of a FERC 
requirement that also included regulation compensation and long-term congestion rights.40   

Each RTO is allocated property rights on market-to-market constraints.  These are known as firm 

flow entitlements (FFE), and each RTO calculates its real-time usage, known as market flow.  

RTOs exchange money (market-to-market settlements) for redispatch based on the non-
monitoring RTO’s41 market flow in relation to its firm flow entitlement.  The non-monitoring RTO 

receives money from the monitoring RTO if its market flow is below its firm flow entitlement.  It 

pays if above its firm flow entitlement.  Figure 2–43 shows payments by month between SPP and 

MISO (positive is payment from MISO to SPP and negative is payment from SPP to MISO.)

control indicators, etc.) and using the RTO with the more economic redispatch to relieve congestion.

2.8  VIRTUAL TRADING 

Market participants in SPP’s Integrated Marketplace may submit virtual energy offers and bids at 

any settlement location in the day-ahead market.  Virtual offers represent energy sales to the 

day-ahead market that the participant needs to buy back in the real-time market.  These are 

referred to as “increment offers,” which are like generation.  Virtual bids represent energy 

purchases in the day-ahead market that the participant needs to sell back in the real-time 

State of the Market 2023 

59 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 2 
Load and resources 

market.  These are referred to as “decrement bids,” which are like load.  The value of virtual 

trading lies in its potential to converge day-ahead and real-time market prices and improve day-

ahead unit commitment decisions. 

For virtual transactions to converge prices, there must be sufficient competition in virtual 

trading; transparency in day-ahead market, reliability unit commitment, and real-time market 

operating practices; and predictability of market events.  Since the market began in 2014, there 

has been increasing levels of virtual participation.  Figure 2–45 displays the total volume of 

virtual transactions as a percentage of real-time market load along with wind output levels.   

Figure 2–45  Cleared virtual transactions as percent of real-time load

Cleared offers as a percent of load

As shown in the figure, virtual transactions averaged 26 percent of real-time market load in 

2023, compared to 29 percent in 2022 and 25 percent in 2021.  Cleared virtual offers as a 

percentage of load amounted to just over 15 percent, down from 18 percent in 2022 and 16 

percent in 2021.  Virtual cleared bids decreased slightly from 12 percent in 2022 to 11 percent in 

2023, while only averaging nine percent in 2021.  Virtual bids typically increase during high load 

hours.  

At nearly 30 percent of load, the average hourly total volume of cleared virtuals ranged from 3.6 

GW of withdrawal to 5.6 GW of injection.  Net cleared virtual positions in the market averaged 

about 1.9 GW of injection, or supply, each hour – a one percent increase year-over-year.

The cleared demand bids that offered more than $30/MWh over the cleared day-ahead price, 

and the supply offers offered at less than $30/MWh under the cleared day-ahead price, are 

considered “price-insensitive.”  Price-insensitive bids and offers are willing to buy/sell at a much 

higher/lower price that could lead to price divergence rather than competitive, or price-

sensitive, bids and offers leading to price convergence between the day-ahead and real-time 

markets.  Price-insensitive bids and offers usually occur at locations with congestion and 

arbitrage against the day-ahead and real-time price differences.  Given that price-insensitive 

bids and offers are likely to clear, these can be unprofitable if congestion around these locations 

does not materialize, leading to divergence between the markets.  Compared to 2022, price-

insensitive bids and price-insensitive offers decreased by over 27 percent and 38 percent 

respectively.  Cleared bids decreased by nearly 10 percent and cleared offers decreased by 15 

percent.

In 2023, nine months were profitable in aggregate for virtual transactions with January, June, 

and July being the exceptions.  In the 118 months since the market began, only 18 months have 

had a net loss when factoring in fees.  Three factors that led to virtual trading losses in January, 

June, and July were low wind generation, low load, and relatively higher day-ahead and real-

time make-whole payments.  Typically, virtual trading profits are higher during periods of high 

wind and low load which creates large price differences between the day-ahead and real-time 

markets.  These price differences stem, in part, from under-cleared wind in the day-ahead 

market.  This contributes to a higher portion of negative prices in real-time as compared to the 
day-ahead.44
