Virtual trades profited $186 million before charges and fees in 2023, nearly a 50 percent 

decrease from 2022.  Virtual bids can be charged distribution fees for day-ahead make-whole 

payments and virtual offers are susceptible to real-time make-whole payment distribution fees.  

In addition, both types of transactions can receive revenue neutrality uplift charge/credits and 

are assessed a per megawatt virtual fee.  The average 2023 rates per megawatt for day-ahead 

make-whole payments, real-time make-whole payments, and real-time revenue neutrality uplift 

distributions are $0.11/MWh, $1.21/MWh, and $0.34/MWh, respectively.  When factoring in

in 2023 decreased by over 35 percent from $116.4 million in 2022.

Six participants accounted for just under 45 percent of the virtual profits after fees, which can 

also be referred to as net profits.  These participants account for roughly 20 percent of the 

transactional volume in the market.  Virtual trading generated profits before fees for 87 

State of the Market 2023 

66 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 2 
Load and resources 

participants, and profits after fees for 73 participants.  The total losses after fees amounted to 

roughly $16.2 million, and six entities accounted for nearly 63 percent of that loss.  

Additionally, Figure 2–52 highlights the disparity in the trading fees paid by each market 

participant.  These fees totaled over $110.6 million in 2023; they include:  virtual fees (two 

percent), real-time revenue neutrality uplift fees (23 percent), day-ahead make-whole payment 

fees (seven percent), and real-time make-whole payment fees (68 percent).  Virtual bids are 

subject to virtual fees, real-time revenue neutrality fees, and day-ahead make-whole payment 

fees.  Virtual offers are subject to virtual fees, real-time revenue neutrality fees, and real-time 

make-whole payment fees.  Nearly three-quarters of the total fees assessed to virtual 

transactions are assessed only to virtual offers.   

The discrepancy in virtual fees relates to the quantity calculation associated with payers of real-

time make-whole payments – specifically, the real-time net settlement location deviation hourly 

amount.  This determinant accounted for over 88 percent of the real-time make-whole 

payments in 2023, or roughly $66 million.  As the name implies, the quantity applied to 

applicable non-virtual transactions includes only the incremental deviations from day-ahead, 

however the quantities assessed to virtual offers include the full virtual offer quantity.   

This calculation methodology, when combined with the larger make-whole payments normally 

associated with real-time, generally leads to higher fees associated with virtual offers when 

compared to virtual bids.  In 2023, the fees associated with virtual offers amounted to $2.47 per 

megawatt compared to $1.24 per megawatt for virtual bids.  This calculation methodology and 

associated incentives could be part of the reason why virtual trading offsets only part of the 

under-clearing of wind resources in the day-ahead market and should be considered as part of 

any analysis or evaluated as part of any potential solution to address price divergence.  The 

market monitor will continue to evaluate these trends going forward.  

Cross-product market manipulation has been a concern in other RTO/ISO markets, and 

extensive monitoring is in place to detect potential cases in the SPP market.  For example, a 

market participant may submit a virtual transaction intended to create congestion that benefits 

a transmission congestion right position.  Generally, this behavior shows up as a loss in one 

market, such as a virtual position, and a substantial associated benefit in another market, such as 

a transmission congestion right position.  In the SPP market, only 12 market participants lost 

more than $100,000 in virtual transactions before fees, and 22 lost more than $100,000 in virtual 

transactions after fees in 2023.  The market monitor reviews these outcomes and takes actions 

as needed.

Market Monitoring Unit 

3  UNIT COMMITMENT AND DISPATCH PROCESSES  

This chapter covers unit commitment and dispatch, scarcity pricing, ramp, and outages.  Key 

points from this chapter include: 

•  Participation in the day-ahead market continues to be robust, though there remains a 

significant resource gap, primarily driven by wind, that creates inefficiencies between 

day-ahead commitments and real-time output. 

•  There remains a significant amount of capacity that self-commits.  While allowed, self-

commitments place constraints on SPP’s ability to minimize production cost and tend to 

lower the marginal cost of energy by shifting the supply curve.  

•  Physical parameters play a notable role in SPP’s ability to efficiently commit and dispatch 

resources.  These parameters can be used to exert market power and potentially limit the 

benefits ratepayers receive from the market.  

•  Fast-start pricing, implemented in 2022, does not seem to have the desired effect of 

clearing more fast-start megawatts in real-time to respond to ramp and uncertainty 

needs, nor does it seem to have a measurable, significant impact on revenues or make-

whole payments.  

•  The number of intervals with scarcity pricing dropped from 2022, particularly for ramp 

up.  During these intervals, the market was generally able to procure the majority of 

energy it needed.  Average scarcity prices were lower across all products.   

•  The ramp and uncertainty products both seem to have design flaws that impact their 

efficacy.  In both cases, pricing tends to be too low, or, in the case of uncertainty, the 

market only prices the product about 20 percent of the time.  Opportunity cost-based 

pricing also creates potential deliverability issues as those resources with the lowest 

opportunity costs tend to be behind constraints.  

•  Availability of accredited resources has continued to trend downward.  Extreme weather 

events continue to demonstrate SPP’s exposure to significant availability risk and 

dependence on capacity that is not fully controllable, including unaccredited wind and 

imports.  Forced outages play a key role in resource unavailability.  For several 

technology types, these outages tend to be highly correlated.

State of the Market 2023 

69 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

3.1  COMMITMENT PROCESS 

The Integrated Marketplace uses centralized unit commitment to determine an efficient 

scheduling and dispatch of generation resources to meet energy demand and operating reserve 

requirements.  Most commitments begin in the day-ahead market.  The day-ahead market 

attempts to commit sufficient capacity to meet the loads that were bid into the day-ahead 

market.  Because of differences between day-ahead and real-time and locational issues, it is 

often necessary to commit additional capacity outside the day-ahead market.  This is done 

through the reliability unit commitment (RUC) processes and manual commitments.  SPP 

employs five reliability commitment processes: 

•  multi-day reliability assessment (MDRA); 

•  day-ahead reliability unit commitment (DA RUC) process; 

• 

• 

intra-day reliability unit commitment (ID RUC) process; 

short-term intra-day reliability unit commitment (ST RUC) process; and 

•  manual commitment instructions issued by the RTO. 

Figure 3–1 shows a timeline describing when SPP executes each commitment processes. 

Figure 3–1  Commitment process timeline 

Multi-day reliability assessments are made for at least three days prior to an operating day.  This 

assessment determines if any long lead-time generators are needed for capacity or are needed 

to address an emergency for the operating day.  Any generator committed from this process is 

treated as a “must commit” in the day-ahead market.  The day-ahead closes at 0930 Central 

time and is executed on the day before the operating day, with the results posted no later than 

1300.  The day-ahead reliability unit commitment process is executed approximately 45 minutes 

after the posting of the day-ahead market results.  This allows market participants time to re-

offer their uncommitted resources, often with better information on forecasts and gas markets.   

The intra-day reliability unit commitment process is run throughout the operating day, with at 

least one execution occurring every four hours.  The short-term intra-day reliability unit 

commitment may be executed as needed to assess resource adequacy over the next two-hour 

State of the Market 2023 

70 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

period as part of the intra-day process.  SPP operators may also issue manual commitment 

instructions for capacity, transmission, or local reliability issues during the operating day to 

address reliability needs not fully reflected in the security constrained unit commitment 

algorithm used in the day-ahead and reliability unit commitment processes.  Transmission 

operators occasionally also initiate local reliability commitments.   

  RESOURCE STARTS 

The SPP resource fleet, excluding variable energy resources, started just over 3.8 million MW of 

capacity in 2023.  That represents an 11 percent decrease from 2022.  The major contributors to 

the decrease in started capacity came from resources started by the day-ahead market.  Figure 

3–2 shows the percentage of capacity from starts by commitment process.  For all generation 

participation offers in the day-ahead market by commitment status, see Figure 3–.

As shown above, 56 percent of started capacity in 2023 was a result of the day-ahead market, 

which continues to be the primary commitment process.  The day-ahead market is the preferred 

method of start-up for resources with longer lead times.  However, a limiting factor on the 

number of day-ahead commitments is that the optimization algorithm is restricted to a 48-hour

window.49  Large base-load resources with long lead-times and long run times may not appear 

economic to the day-ahead market commitment algorithm.  Some market participants choose 

to self-commit these resources at their economic minimum in order to have them online and 

available for incremental dispatch.   

Within the operating day, commitment flexibility is limited by resource start-up times.  As the 

operating hour approaches, fewer resources are eligible to be started.  The reliability unit 
commitment processes—day-ahead,50 intra-day, short-term, and manual—represent about 18 

percent of the started capacity.  Day-ahead RUC starts are evenly distributed throughout the 

day, filling the resource gap caused by virtual transactions and wind as well as forecast 

differences.  Intra-day RUC starts occur mostly in the midday hours between 11 am and 3 pm, 

covering peak hours.  The short-term RUC starts increase until late evening.  On top of resource 

and forecast uncertainty, RUC commitments also fill any need for additional ramp-able capacity.  

The ramp product, implemented in March 2022, and the uncertainty product, implemented in 
July 2023, were expected to help reduce these amounts.51    

Figure 3–3 shows that a large majority of start-up instructions issued to combined-cycle 

generators are the result of the day-ahead market.  This result is expected given the lower 

variable costs and more flexible operating parameters for these resources relative to other gas 

units.

down a resource at the end of one day only to start it an hour later the next day).

  SELF-COMMITMENT 

The SPP market allows participants to self-commit resources rather than have the market choose 

which units to run.  While there may be good reasons for this, the practice can distort prices, 

offer and bid behavior, market outcomes, and investment signals. 

State of the Market 2023 

73 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

The volume of dispatched megawatts from self-committed resources remains nearly one-third 
of the total dispatch megawatt volumes.52  In other words, nearly one-third of the energy 

produced in 2023 was from a resource that was not economically selected by the day-ahead 
market’s centralized unit commitment process.53  Self-commitment shifts the merit order of the 

supply curve by treating the self-committed generators as price insensitive at their minimum, 

which shifts the supply curve to the right.  The expected result of a rightward shift in supply is a 

decline in the marginal price of energy. 

Figure 3–4 shows dispatch megawatts by technology type by commitment type for each year of 

the study period.   

Figure 3–4  Dispatch megawatt hours by technology type and commitment type

While resources of various technology types self-commit, coal resources have produced and 

continue to account for the largest portion of self-committed megawatts.  Wind, nuclear, and 

hydro resources make up the vast majority of the remaining self-committed megawatts.  In 

terms of total megawatt hours committed, coal and wind resources saw decreases of 17 and 10 

percent from 2022 while nuclear resources saw an increase of 14 percent from 2022.   

Resource lead-times, also called start-up times, are time-based operational parameters that vary 

widely by technology type.  In the Integrated Marketplace, resources can submit three different

lead times:  cold, intermediate, and hot.  Thermal resources generally have longer lead times 

when they are cold as opposed to when they are hot.  In the following section, lead times by 

commitment status and technology type are examined.

Self-committed resources tend to have longer lead times than market-committed resources.  

Because the centralized unit commitment must observe constraints other than cost, such as lead 

time, it may continue to run a unit even when the marginal price falls below that unit’s offer.  

Nuclear units have the longest cold start-up time, followed by coal and natural gas. 

Start-up offers are generally representative of the cost that a market participant incurs when 

starting a generating unit from an off-line state to its economic minimum and the cost to 

eventually shut the unit down.  These offers are submitted in terms of dollars per start.  Many of 

the units with high start-up costs have minimum run times that extend past the day-ahead 

market window.  If the optimization evaluated start-up costs over each resource’s full minimum 

run time, their start-up offers would be more competitive with shorter lead-time resources.  

Somewhat like lead-time, coal units have the highest cold start-up cost, followed by nuclear and 

natural gas.   

By reducing self-commitment, prices and investment signals will likely be less distorted.  A 

smaller distortion will likely help market participants make better short-run and long-run 

decisions, which tends to coincide with improved market efficiency and profit maximization.   

While the MMU has seen gradual reductions in self-commitments over the last few years, 

generation from self-committed generators still represents about one-third of the generation in 

the SPP market.   

State of the Market 2023 

75 

 
  
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Given its significance, the MMU recommends that the SPP and its stakeholders continue to find 

ways to further reduce self-commitments including developing a multi-day economic 
assessment.54  SPP and stakeholders are currently developing a process for multi-day economic 

commitment.  While the MMU generally supports this initiative as working to address the MMU 

self-commitment concern, the MMU does have outstanding concerns regarding how 

participants can influence commitments through offers.  The proposed process creates a 

potential gaming opportunity that was addressed with how SPP addresses resources with multi-

day minimum runtimes.   

  PHYSICAL PARAMETERS 

The purpose of the centralized unit commitment processes is to commit sufficient resources to 

serve load, subject to transmission and resource constraints, while minimizing cost.  The 

centralized unit commitment process can minimize commitment costs because it has 

information, such as the amount of capacity available, the current transmission topology, and 

the operational parameters of each resource.  To the latter, SPP allows participants to self-report 

the physical operating parameters of each resource.  Per the market protocols, these physical 

operating parameters should reflect the underlying physical capabilities of a resource.  

A resource’s physical parameters determine how often and how quickly a resource can be 

started, how long a resource must run once online, and how quickly a resource can change its 

output (ramp), among other things.  Resources with physical parameters that limit how quickly it 

can change its output and/or its availability for commitment or dispatch are, in general, less 

flexible.  Moreover, a resource’s physical parameters can indicate what services, outside of 

energy, a resource can provide the market.  This includes regulation, ramp, uncertainty, 

contingency, and quick-start capability.  The growth in variable energy resources has increased 

the demand for ancillary services, such as regulation, ramp, and uncertainty, and requires a 

system with more flexibility to respond to sudden changes in output.  Thus, to be effective and 

efficient, an accurate representation of the physical capabilities for resources continues to grow 

in importance. 

Figure 3-6 provides a summary of the average values for a subset of physical parameters across 

the fleet.  Additionally, as resources with a common fuel frequently have relatively similar 

physical capabilities, the table also shows these values by fuel type.   

54 Chapter 7, recommendation 2017.4 “Address inefficiency caused by self-committed resources” for more

The two nuclear units are the least flexible with almost no dispatchable range (economic 

maximum minus economic minimum).  Coal units, which are generally older steam turbines, 

making up roughly 22 percent of the region’s installed capacity, have long startup times, slow 

ramp rates, and long minimum run times.  Coal resources are aging out of the fleet and will 

represent a diminishing share of capacity in the region in the coming years.  

Natural gas resources, which consist of a combination of steam and combustion turbines, have 

much shorter startup times, faster ramp rates, and an average minimum run time of just shy of 

one day.  These resources are much more flexible and account for roughly 37 percent of the 

region’s installed capacity. The most flexible resources are solar, water, and wind resources and, 

to a slightly lesser degree, oil resources.  Together, these resources make up roughly 38 percent 

of the region’s installed capacity.  Of the conventional resources, natural gas units, particularly 

combustion turbines, are the most likely to increase in installed capacity.  Wind and solar are 

expected to see the most growth, as a percentage of the total installed capacity, in the coming 

years.  However, though providing the most flexibility and highest ramp rates, they represent the 

highest uncertainty in available capacity and are the least controllable by SPP.   

In an efficient market, physical parameters should reflect the physical capabilities of a resource.  

However, because physical parameters are self-reported, opportunities exist to set physical 

parameters in such a way as to significantly influence or hedge against certain outcomes related 

to commitment and dispatch.  Examples of this behavior that the MMU has observed include: 

State of the Market 2023 

77 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

•  Changing real-time economic minimum operating limits in the real-time balancing 

market to match day-ahead commitment and clearing.  This allows resources to avoid 

being dispatched below their day-ahead clearing in real-time to serve a contract or 

hedge against buying back their day-ahead position at a loss while ramp-constrained.  

The MMU observed 126 resources, in market status in real-time, engaging in this 

behavior.  This was observed for a small percentage of hours for most resources; 

however, a handful of resources engaged in this behavior for a measurable proportion of 

the year. 

•  Depressing ramp-down rates to avoid becoming ramp constrained and missing price 

spikes.  This allows inframarginal resources to avoid being ramped down during a 

transient downward price excursion, becoming ramp constrained and missing out on 

producer surplus or potentially buying back a lucrative day-ahead position at a loss, 

when prices rebound.  Limiting ramp rates creates unnecessary inflexibility in the fleet of 

resources managed by SPP, and leads to an inefficient, more costly, dispatch solution.  

•  Extending minimum runtimes beyond the day-ahead economic evaluation window (two 

days).  The day-ahead market solution minimizes forecasted production costs, including 

uplift, across a two-day window to help normalize production costs across resource 

types.  Resources with more expensive starts may be the best economic choice when 

evaluated over the two-day period.  However, if the resource with a minimum runtime 

longer than two days is no longer economic after the evaluation period, it is treated as a 

“must run” for the remainder of its minimum runtime and is eligible to be made whole to 

its mitigated costs.  The minimum runtime parameter can be used to ensure a resource 

meets contractual obligations or to burn its fuel as a hedge against a take or pay 

contract. 

Using physical parameters to effectively override what would otherwise be the outcome of the 

central commitment and dispatch process leads to sub-optimal, inefficient market outcomes.  

Moreover, this behavior can be a means to exert market power by changing the quantity and 

potentially the price of energy, but not to a degree that would fail SPP’s conduct and impact 

test.  As such, the MMU maintains that physical parameters should reflect the current underlying 

physical capabilities of a resource and that this should be included in the tariff, not just the 

market protocols.   

State of the Market 2023 

78 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

  FAST-START RESOURCES COMMITMENT 

In 2016, FERC issued a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking (NOPR) on fast-start pricing processes as 

a part of a broad initiative aimed at improving price formation in regulated wholesale power 
markets in the United States.55  This was followed by an order published in December 2017 
which specifically targeted SPP’s fast-start pricing, finding it unjust and unreasonable.56  As a 

result, SPP filed proposed changes to its pricing practices, which were accepted by FERC in June 
2019 with an implementation date of May 2022.57   

As of May 18, 2022, a fast-start resource is defined as any dispatchable resource that can start, 

synchronize, begin injecting energy within 10 minutes of SPP notification, and have a minimum-

run time of one hour or less.  Market storage resources with a discharge time of 60 minutes or 

less also meet the fast-start criteria.  Prior to this date, dispatchable resources with a start-time 

of less than 10 minutes and choosing to classify as quick-starts were considered quick-start 

resources, regardless of run time requirements.  

The changes to permissible costs in energy offers for fast-start resources resulted in the 

formulation of two new adders that reflect amortized start-up and no-load costs, respectively.  

These adders are described by the formula below:

Functionally, the two adders translate dollar-denominated startup and no-load costs into dollar 

per megawatt-hour costs that the market-clearing engine can integrate into and interpret as 

incremental production costs.  Combined with the existing incremental energy offer, the three 

components sum to a “composite offer” specifically employed for fast-start qualified resources.

Total make-whole payments to fast-start resources were roughly $20 million dollars, with nearly 

$3 million of that coming from the day-ahead market.  This is a 48 percent decrease from 2022, 

largely driven by fewer make-whole payments paid to natural gas units.  Much of this change is 

likely due to the sharp decline in gas prices from 2022.  Day-ahead make-whole payments per 

megawatt eligible for day-ahead cost reimbursement averaged $0.93/MWh for natural gas, and 

State of the Market 2023 

81 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

$1.38/MWh for hydro.  These metrics for real-time were $416/MWh for fuel oil, $21/MWh for 

natural gas, and $1/MWh for hydro.  Real-time make-whole payments, while decreasing overall 

by 33 percent, went up 31 percent for fuel oil resources.  

There are many elements that can affect make-whole payments to include the frequency of the 

resources’ deployments, the associated market prices, and the resources’ fuel costs.  The fast-

start logic did not have a direct effect on the total operating cost of fast-start resources, but it 

does influence the revenues those resources can receive. 

As described above, offline fast-start resources are cleared and dispatched based on their 

energy offers and submitted resource parameters.  However, they can set higher prices in the 

pricing run based off their composite offers, described above.  Make-whole payments are 

composed of resource’s costs compared to revenues during a commitment period.  Because the 

fast-start logic did not affect the costs that resources are made-whole to, changes in revenues 

will be the only driver for the changes in make-whole payments.  

Prior to the fast-start logic, resources’ energy day-ahead revenues were calculated by 

multiplying the day-ahead locational marginal price in the day-ahead dispatch-run by the 

megawatts generated in the day-ahead dispatch run.  After the fast-start logic, resources are 

compensated by multiplying the day-ahead dispatch megawatt from the day-ahead dispatch-

run times to the pricing-run’s applicable day-ahead locational marginal price.  These methods 

apply to both day-ahead and real-time markets, except in real-time day-ahead megawatts-

cleared are subtracted from the real-time and multiply the product by the real-time prices.  

Since implementation, there has been very little change in the revenues to fast-start units due to 

the new fast-start pricing.  All else equal, the increase in revenue would cause a negligible 

reduction in make-whole payments. 

  GENERATION SCHEDULING 

The day-ahead market provides market participants with the ability to submit offers to sell 

energy, regulation-up service, regulation-down service, spinning reserve, and supplemental 

reserve, and/or to submit bids to purchase energy.  The day-ahead market co-optimizes the 

clearing of energy and operating reserve products out of the offered capacity.  All day-ahead 

market products are traded and settled on an hourly basis. 

In 2023, participation in the day-ahead market dropped.  Load-serving entities that also own 

generation assets consistently offered generation into the day-ahead market at levels in excess 

of the requirements of the limited day-ahead must-offer obligation.  Participation by merchant 

generation—for which no such obligation exists—was significantly higher for fossil fuel

resources, but considerably lower for variable energy resources.  Figure 3– also shows that 

merchant generators self-commit fossil fuel resources at a much lower rate than load-serving 

entities.  This is likely because merchant generators have incentive structures in place based 

primarily on market outcomes.

While having excess capacity online compared to load is necessary, having too much capacity 

online can lead to inefficiencies.  Beginning in 2016, capacity as a percent of load decreased 

yearly through 2018.  However, from 2018 through 2020, there were small increases, with 2020 

averaging 123 percent.  Capacity commitment as a percent of load dropped in 2021 to 120 

percent and has been increasing once again since bottoming out in 2021.   

In 2023, online capacity as a percent of load averaged 123 percent compared to 120 and 121 

percent in 2021 and 2022, respectively.  Having too much capacity on-line with a non-zero 

minimum causes other resources to operate lower on their offer curves, which can contribute to 

under-recovery of costs and, therefore, increased make-whole payments.  The downward 

pressure on prices can also mute the signal for investments in new capacity.   

Additional capacity may be beneficial for necessary rampable capacity.  Prior to the ramp 

product, which was implemented in March 2022, there was no rampable capacity requirement 

other than instantaneously load capacity, which has a reserved use.  The uncertainty product 

State of the Market 2023 

84 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

along with the ramp product are anticipated to help supply rampable capacity to the market.58  

Prior to these products, the market clearing software had no method for optimizing or pricing 

the clearing of rampable capacity for a given interval.  The MMU has concerns, outlined in 

Sections 3.2.4 and 3.2.5, for both products that will need to be addressed for the effective 

delivery of procured ramp. 

  MUST-OFFER PROVISION 

The Integrated Marketplace has a limited day-ahead must-offer provision that was intended to 

incentivize load-serving entities with generation assets to participate in the day-ahead market.  

Market participants that are non-compliant are assessed a penalty based on the amount of 

capacity available in the day-ahead market relative to the market participant’s peak hourly real-

time load.  The requirement is limited in the sense that not all resources or capacity must be 

offered.  Only market participants with generation assets that serve load are subject to the must-

offer requirement, and they are required to offer enough generation to cover most of their load 

plus reserve obligations, per asset owner.  An alternative way to satisfy the provision is to offer 

all resource capacity not on outage.  Compared to 2022 where no day-ahead must-offer 

penalties were assessed, two asset owners were assessed penalties for a total of $49,745 in 

must-offer penalties.  The must-offer violations occurred over 41 distinct days.   

While this provision does highly encourage available generation to be offered, it does not 

impose a penalty for excessive outages, which has been cited as a reason for conservative 

operations in the past.  The day-ahead must-offer provision also does not tie into Attachment 

AA, which defines the resource adequacy requirement in the SPP tariff.   

The MMU continues to recommend updating the day-ahead must offer requirement and 

addressing FERC’s concerns.  See further discussion in Section 7.4. 

3.2  DISPATCH 

  DEMAND FOR RELIABILITY 

Figure 3–2 noted that about 18 percent of SPP start-up instructions by capacity originated from 

SPP reliability unit commitment processes.  To understand the need for reliability commitments, 

it is useful to discuss the different assumptions, requirements, and rules that are used in the 

reliability unit commitment processes after the day-ahead market. 

58 The ramp product is covered in Section 3.2.4, below.  SPP implemented an uncertainty product in 2023, 
covered in Section 3.2.5.   

State of the Market 2023 

85 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

One difference between day-ahead and real-time is wind generation.  Seventy-seven percent of 

real-time wind production cleared in the day-ahead market in 2023, a significant decrease from 

the eighty-eight percent that cleared in the day-ahead market in 2022.  Market participants 

determine the participation levels for their wind resources in the day-ahead market through 

supply offers.  In contrast, the reliability unit commitment processes use SPP’s wind forecast.   

Another important difference between the two studies is virtual transactions.  Market 

participants submit virtual bids to buy and virtual offers to sell energy in the day-ahead market.  

A virtual transaction is not tied to an obligation to generate or consume energy; rather, it is a 

financial instrument that is cleared by taking the opposite position in the real-time market.  

Because the reliability unit commitment processes must ensure sufficient generation is on-line 

to meet energy demand, virtual transactions are not included in the reliability unit commitment 

processes used in day-ahead, intra-day, or short-term. 

Other differences also affect net energy demand.  Net energy demand is demand net of both 

variable energy generation, the combination of imports, exports, and parallel flows from other 

markets.  Import and export transaction data are updated to include the latest information 

available for the reliability unit commitment processes.  A fundamental difference between the 

two studies is the definition of demand.  In the day-ahead market, demand is determined by 

bids submitted by the market participants whereas, in the real-time market, demand is physical.  

Demand bids in the day-ahead market average around 98 percent of the real-time values, as 

shown in Figure 2–5.  Other smaller differences between the two markets include losses and 

operating reserves. 

These differences are referred to as resource gaps (i.e., a gap in meeting demand) between the 

day-ahead and real-time markets.  The resource gap is the excess price-following, physical 

generation cleared in the day-ahead market that was not needed in real-time.  A negative 

resource gap would indicate that the total generation cleared in the day-ahead market is 

insufficient to serve real-time demand.  The resource gap is typically positive, indicating more 

dispatchable generation is cleared in the day-ahead market than was necessary to serve real-

time load. 

The primary drivers for the resource gaps are:   

1)  differences in virtual supply net of virtual demand,  

2)  differences in real-time wind generation compared to wind cleared in the day-ahead 

market, and 

3)  real-time net exports exceeding day-ahead net exports.   

State of the Market 2023 

86 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

It is generally true that the day-ahead market clears less wind generation than is produced in 

real-time.  The mismatch is partly because some market participants with wind generation 

assets, recognizing the uncertainty of the wind forecast in day-ahead, offered a percent of the 

forecasted capacity or, in some cases, choose not to participate in the day-ahead market at all.  

This may cause other generation to clear in the day-ahead market that will not be needed in 

real-time when the wind replaces it.  

The resource gaps can help explain why some generators produce much less in real-time or why 

additional commitments occur after the day-ahead market has cleared.  Figure 3–15 compares 

on-line capacity between the day-ahead and real-time markets alongside the average prices.

 Wind gap

Virtuals gap

Resource gap

In 2021, the resource gap averaged 4,500 megawatts whereas by 2023, the resource gap 

averaged just under 3,500 megawatts, a drop of 23 percent.  Despite the drop, the main driver 

of the resource gap, the wind gap, has remained flat since 2021.  The wind gap averaged around 

2,700 megawatts.  By contrast, the virtuals gap – the second largest contributor to the resource 

gap - decreased by 24 percent while the net load gap has increased.  This explains how during 

July, August, and September, the wind gap was able to exceed the resource gap overall.  

The wind gap can be caused by insufficient clearing of wind generation in the day-ahead 

market, either due to forecast error or due to wind generator owners deliberately offering in 

significantly less power in the day-ahead market than they produce in real-time.  To balance this 

insufficiency, the day-ahead market may clear other generation or virtual offers.  In real-time, 

actual wind generation replaces the additional dispatchable generation and virtual offers that 

cleared in day-ahead.  The result can be that the day-ahead market clears excess generation 

which can lead to certain resources being run at their minimum or lower on their offer curve in 

real-time due to minimum run time and other operational constraints.  This, in turn, can lower 

the real-time energy price, making real-time prices diverge from day-ahead prices. 

Figure 3–17 also demonstrates correlation between the shapes of the wind gap, virtuals gap, 

and the shape of the overall resource gap.  This further indicates that the wind and virtuals gap 

are the primary driver of the total resource gap.  As discussed in Section 2.8, virtuals occur 

mostly at wind generator locations.  The effect of this is seen in Figure 3–17 as the shape of the 

virtual gap largely follows the shape of the wind gap.  This indicates that, on average, the 

virtuals are reacting to the changes in wind generation.  

State of the Market 2023 

88 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

On average, the market-wide resource gap is positive, and no additional capacity is needed in 

real-time.  However, in some cases, the day-ahead market can clear insufficient generation.  This 

can be a case that is not represented by the average, a locational insufficiency due to 

congestion, or a parameter that is not directly or sufficiently cleared in the day-ahead market 

such as ramp.  When the day-ahead market clears insufficient generation, additional capacity 

may be committed for reliability after day-ahead. 

One of the reasons for reliability commitments is the need for ramp capability.  The 

instantaneous load capacity constraint may commit additional resources to ensure there is 

adequate ramping capacity to meet the instantaneous peak demand for any given hour.  The 

instantaneous load capacity constraint is defined as the greater of the forecasted instantaneous 

peak load, or an SPP defined default value.  Figure 3–17 shows the percentage of hours for 

which the default value is used for the upper bound and lower bound of instantaneous load 

capacity.

A value is calculated for upper bound (upward ramp) and a lower bound (downward ramp) 

based on forecasted load.  However, the default, or minimum requirement, is not based on 

market information.  Because the default value is used in over half of all intervals, the 

instantaneous load capacity constraint can contribute to reliability commitments that are not 

based on current market information.  The default requirements are hourly values as low as 200 

MW.  SPP evaluates the default values quarterly.

Head-room requirement (MW)

The most frequent observations were from 500 MW to 599 MW at around 22.5 percent of the 

observations.  This represents a shift upward from 2022, which saw more observations of the 

head-room requirement fall between 300 MW and 499 MW.  While a market-based product is 

more appropriate for a market efficiency improvement, keeping this requirement low may help 

reduce unnecessary make-whole payments.  A continued shift upward in the default 

requirement may diminish any possible benefit of lowering unnecessary make-whole payments.  

Resources committed to provide ramp capability can affect real-time prices, whether because of 

applying the instantaneous load capacity constraint in a reliability unit commitment process or a 

manual process.  Without the appropriate scarcity pricing rules that reflect the market value of 

capacity shortages due to ramp capability, the cost of bringing the resource on-line may not be 

fully reflected in the real-time prices.  The resource keeping the market from being scarce may 

not be paid to provide the needed capability.  Additionally, manual commitments made during 

conservative operations, while possibly needed for capacity, similarly suppress the price signals 

when they are needed most.  

Reliability commitments, along with wind exceeding the day-ahead forecast, can dampen real-

time price signals, as is evidenced by 55 percent of real-time make-whole payments made for 
reliability unit commitments, as shown in Figure 4–28.59

  RAMPING 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

The increase or decrease of the resource’s output to achieve the next dispatch instruction is 

called “ramp.”  The number of megawatts a resource can ramp in one minute is the resource’s 

“ramp rate.” 

In real-time, resources are increasing and decreasing output to meet changes in both load and 

non-dispatchable generation.  These changes can be measured as changes in net load.  Net load 

is net of non-dispatchable generation, net imports/exports, and parallel flows from other 

markets. 

Figure 3–19 shows the frequency and extent of net load changes from one real-time interval to 

the next.

Interval to interval net load change

Of the net load changes between real-time intervals, 95 percent were between a decrease of 

about 394 MW and an increase of about 377 MW.  This is up from 2022 when the decrease was 
about 381 MW and the increase was about 359 MW.  The 95th percentile net load changes, both 

positive and negative, increased by about two percent in 2023.  This was lower than the nine 

percent increase from 2021 to 2022 and the eight percent increase between 2020 to 2021. 

These numbers highlight a slow increase in net load swings.  These changes in net load must be 

balanced by resources with a flexible dispatch range, or ramp capability.

Net load volatility increased about nine percent from 2021 to 2022 and about one percent from 

2022 to 2023.  Net load volatility has steadily increased since 2014.  

As variable energy resources serve more load, volatility is expected to increase.  Variable energy 

resources, primarily wind and solar, produced about 37 percent of total generation in 2023.  Of 

all intervals where these resources were on-line and available for economic dispatch, forecasted 

variable energy resources were expected to follow dispatch in about eight percent of resource-

intervals, compared to about 80 percent for non-forecasted energy resources.  When these 

forecasted resources are not following dispatch, it is possible for them to reduce the ramp need 

when they move in the same direction as demand, as long as they don’t overshoot demand by 

too much.  However, they can also increase the amount of ramp need when they move in the 

opposite direction as demand or overshoot demand by a large quantity.  When they increase 

the system ramp need, flexible, dependable resources are needed to account for the additional 

ramp they cause.

to undispatched, forecasted variable energy resources with the ramp that would have been 

needed for demand alone.

Demand

When forecasted resources were not following dispatch, they increased the real-time system net 
ramp need in about 73 percent of intervals.  The 95th percentile increase in net ramp need 

averaged nearly 490 MW for 2023.  The highest month was April, which averaged just over 670 
MW.  This is more than twice the ramp needed to meet the 95th percentile demand alone in 

these months.  In contrast, undispatched forecasted resources decreased net ramp need by only 

about 150 MW at the upper end.  Because the precise timing of the fluctuations of forecasted 

resources is unknown, rampable capacity must be committed and available throughout the day.  

This is even more challenging when the resources’ real-time output does not follow the forecast.  

While forecasted variable energy resources provide inexpensive energy, it comes at the cost of a 

greater need for more system ramp. 

  SCARCITY PRICING 

A scarcity price is a price that reflects the value of a product when there is not enough of the 

product to meet the demand.  SPP’s market uses marginal cost pricing, which prices a product 

by the cost to produce the next increment.  When a product is scarce, there may not be 

additional supply, so price cannot be determined by the next increment.  In this case, a scarcity 

price is used to set marginal price.  The Integrated Marketplace uses demand curves to set 

graduated scarcity prices so that small scarcities are priced lower than large scarcities.  Scarcity 

prices inform market participants that the product was short and incentivize future provision of 

that product. 

State of the Market 2023 

93 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

When an insufficient amount of ramp capability-up service, ramp capability-down service, 

regulation-up service, regulation-down service, or contingency reserve is cleared, a scarcity price 

is set by a demand curve.  The scarcity of these products can be caused by a lack of capacity or a 

lack of ramp.  Scarcities are due to capacity when there are insufficient resources at maximum 

output available to meet demand.  Scarcities are due to ramp when sufficient capacity is 

available, but ramp rate limitations do not allow access to the full capacity.  When multiple 

products compete for the same, limited capability of resources, the scarcity of one product can 

also raise the price of other products.  As of May 2022, scarcity prices are set by the pricing run 
according to the dispatch in the pricing run.60  Scarcity or a lack of scarcity in the dispatch run 

has no bearing on scarcity prices in the marketplace. 

Regulation, ramp capability, and contingency reserve scarcities are priced by demand curves.  

The regulation demand curves, for both up and down, consist of six steps with a maximum price 

of $600/MW.  The contingency reserve demand curve consists of three steps with a maximum 

price of $1,100/MW.  The demand curve for ramp capability, for both up and down, consists of 

six steps with the maximum scarcity price is based on the average cost to run a dispatchable 

fast-start resource at its maximum output for its minimum run time.  SPP updates this maximum 

demand curve price monthly based on three months of historical offers.  The highest maximum 

demand curve price was $36/MW.   

The clearing engine does not record the reason for the scarcity, (i.e., capacity or ramp.)  The 

MMU suggests that SPP capture the appropriate information so that the reason for the scarcity 

will be transparent. 

Figure 3–22 displays the number of regulation and contingency reserve scarcity intervals in the 

real-time market and average prices for each product by month, along with an annual average 

of monthly values.  There was no day-ahead scarcity for regulation or contingency reserve in 

2023. 

60  Pricing runs and dispatch runs are products of fast-start logic, discussed in Section 3.1.4.

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

About 21 percent of regulation-up scarcities and about 46 percent of regulation-down scarcities 

occurred in the first interval of the hour, representing a slight increase from 2022.  Regulation 

scarcity events typically happen at the beginning of the hour and have since the inception of the 

marketplace.  Contingency reserve scarcities were more equally distributed across the hour, 

though they also peaked at 16 percent in the first interval of the hour.  Ramp capability-up 

scarcities were more evenly distributed throughout the hour than regulation scarcities.  

However, in 2023, they increase steadily as the interval gets closer to the end of an hour.   

One potential reason for regulation scarcities peaking at the beginning of the hour is that SPP 

does not pre-position regulating resources to be within their regulating maximum and minimum 

limits prior to the period that the resource is cleared for regulation.  Consider a resource that is 

currently dispatched to its minimum of 100 MW.  If this resource clears 20 MW of regulation-

down reserves in the next hour, it will need to move up to 120 MW.  If the resource’s ramp rate 

does not allow it to ramp up 20 MW in one interval, the resource cannot provide regulation-

down in the first interval.  If this causes a scarcity event, the resource may have to buy back a 

day-ahead position at scarcity prices.  However, if the resource moves there prior to the hour, it 
will deviate from its current dispatch instruction, which has financial penalties.61  Consequently, 

resources often follow dispatch until the first interval of the regulation commitment, 

contributing to shortages in the first interval of the regulating commitment. 

 RAMP CAPABILITY PRODUCT 

The volume of scarcity events highlighted in Figure 3–22, illustrates the need for ramp capability.  

SPP has a ramp capability product, which was implemented in March 2022.  While the MMU 

generally supported the proposed design, the MMU did have some concerns with the design 
prior to implementation.62  In addition to these concerns, the MMU identified an issue after 

implementation with the deliverability of ramp cleared by the ramp-up capability product.  The 

MMU pre-implementation concerns and the deliverability concerns are both discussed later in 

this section.   

The MMU’s preliminary findings raise the following concerns with performance of the ramp 

capability product: 

61 Resources that deviate from their dispatch signals can receive Uninstructed Deviated Charges for the 
deviated megawatts.  The weighted average price per megawatt for deviation in 2023 was $2.60, down 
from $3.58/MWh in 2022.  The maximum price charged for deviation was $28.88/MWh, down from the 
$44.20/MWh in 2022.  In addition to this charge, resources do not receive cost reimbursement for any 
deviated megawatts in the event energy prices are lower than energy.  
62 See MMU comments in Docket No. ER20-1617. 

State of the Market 2023 

98 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

1.  Variable energy resources cannot produce regulation-up in the SPP market.  However, 54 

percent of the day-ahead ramp-up and 30 percent of the 2023 real-time ramp-up 
product was cleared on variable energy resources. 

2.  The scarcity demand curve prices for the ramp capability up product may be set too low.  
There is a high frequency of ramp-up scarcity events, especially when taking in the fact 
that non-deployable ramp-up is being cleared to meet the requirement. 

  Ramping limitations affect market outcomes 

The real-time dispatch does not consider future intervals.  It simply calculates one value:  a 

dispatch instruction for the next interval.  While the real-time balancing market considers a 

resource’s ramp capability for the purpose of calculating the dispatch instruction for the next 

interval, ramp is not considered for any interval after that.  Prior to the ramp product’s 

implementation, ramp needs were not accounted for in terms of the subsequent dispatch 

instructions even though ramp is the very capability that allows a resource to get to future 

dispatch instructions. 

Not having ramp capability considered for future intervals can cause an inability in the market 

clearing engine to procure enough energy to serve the load or provide sufficient operating 

reserves in those future intervals.  Even when enough capacity is available, a lack of ramp 

renders that capacity unreachable.  Moreover, sufficient ramp has typically been offered, by the 

market participants, but the clearing process has not left enough available for future use.  When 
this occurs in the pricing run, it often leads to short-term transitory price spikes.63  Real-time 

scarcity events in the dispatch run are shown in Figure 3–27. 

63 This is essentially temporal, or time-based, congestion.

Of all the regulation-up scarcity events, about 61 percent lasted for only one interval, and about 

18 percent lasted for two intervals.  For regulation-down scarcity events, about 72 percent lasted 

for only one interval, and about 17 percent lasted for two intervals.  Operating reserve scarcity 

64 The uncertainty product was introduced in September 2023 and had no day-ahead scarcity and only one 
interval of real-time scarcity during 2023.  

State of the Market 2023 

100 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

event lengths were just slightly more diverse with about 51 percent lasting for one interval, 

about 24 percent lasting two intervals, and about 25 percent lasting three intervals or more.  

This trend is similar in recent years.  For ramp-up scarcity events, 40 percent lasted one interval, 

and 16 percent lasted for two intervals.  While ramp-up scarcity events decrease quickly as event 

length increases, like other products, there were many more ramp-up scarcity events, and they 

lasted longer.  This is mostly due to the product’s low scarcity demand curve prices.  There were 

no ramp-down scarcity events.  There was one uncertainty scarcity event and it lasted one 

interval. 

Where sufficient capacity cannot be dispatched, scarcity prices are invoked.  Scarcity prices are 

economic signals alerting market participants to the insufficient supply of a product.  Almost all 

of these intervals with scarcity pricing were due to a lack of cleared ramp and not a lack of 

capacity.  If sufficient ramp was reserved in advance for these scarcity intervals, then these 

scarcities likely could have been avoided.  Ramp availability increased in previous years, but 

scarcity events have also increased, highlighting the continued need for systematic ramp 

procurement.  The ramp capability product was expected to help reduce these transient scarcity 

events. 

In addition, marginal energy prices can be elevated even when energy is not scarce.  When ramp 

in the up direction is short, energy will always be given the highest priority.  If there is not 

sufficient ramp to meet both energy and regulation-up, for instance, then the regulation-up 

scarcity price will be reflected in the marginal energy price.  This causes a high marginal energy 

price even though there is no energy scarcity because the two products are competing for ramp.  

This makes energy prices more volatile.  If sufficient ramp had been available, then regulation-

up scarcity prices would not have raised the marginal energy price.  A well-designed ramp 

capability product can ensure that more rampable capacity is available to meet energy so that 

regulation-up scarcity prices can be avoided.  This helps to better reflect system conditions and 

reduces dispatch volatility.  

  Design of the ramp capability product 

In the 2017 annual report, the market monitor recommended that SPP create a ramp capability 

product.  SPP implemented the ramp capability product in March 2022. This product is used to 

provision rampable up and down capacity for uncertainty events in net load forecasts across a 

10-minute future time horizon.  Historically, SPP operators procured ramp through out-of-

market mechanisms, often resulting in uplift payments. 

The ramp capability product was designed to allow for more economical and transparent 

management of the intermittent aspects of the system.  The ramp capability product design 

State of the Market 2023 

101 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

optimizes the resources’ dispatch instructions over a ten-minute period to allocate any 

economically available ramp for the interval starting ten minutes in the future.  Future ramp 

needs get met by pre-positioning online resources with available ramp if the cost of this action 

is less than the applicable ramp-scarcity demand curve price.  The ramp requirement is set to 

procure enough ramp to meet forecasted net load changes plus an amount to cover unexpected 

net load changes based on historical needs.  The ramp product optimizes only online ramp.  Off-

line ramp is not eligible to clear the ramp capability product, due to the short 10-minute 

clearing horizon.  A market-clearing price will be set by the opportunity cost of providing other 

products.

megawatt hours for each product by fuel type.

As shown in the two above charts a large percentage of ramp capability-up is cleared by wind 

and natural gas resources.  These figures also show that wind resources clear a greater 

percentage in the day-ahead market than the real-time market.  Sixty-one percent of the ramp 

capability-up cleared in the day-market was cleared by wind, as compared to only 44 percent 

cleared by wind resources in real time. 

In winter 2022, the MMU evaluated the effectiveness of the newly implemented ramp product 

and discovered a design issue.  This issue along with the market monitor’s other pre-
implementation concerns are discussed below:65 

3.2.4.2.1  Ramp capability-up procured behind binding constraints 

The MMU was concerned with the trend of day-ahead ramp capability up scarcity events being 
roughly two and a half times the frequency of real-time ramp capability up scarcity events.66  

This is not a logical trend as there is far less uncertainty in the day-ahead market than the real-

time market.  In the day-ahead market, resources are considered to always stay on dispatch, 

forecasted outputs are assumed to be in line with forecasted quantities, and resources are not 

considered to trip.  These assumptions do not hold true in the real-time market, so typically 

there are far more regulation and operating reserve shortages due to ramp limitations in real-
time than day-ahead.67 

65 See MMU comments in Docket No. ER20-1617. 
66 See Figures 3-13 and 3-14. 
67 See Figures 3-13 and 3-14. 

State of the Market 2023 

103 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

The reason for the disparity between day-ahead and real-time ramp shortages has to do with 

how ramp capability-up is accessed for clearing.  The resource owners do not offer a cost for 

either ramp capability-up or -down.  Instead, the products clear solely based off their 

opportunity costs for not clearing other products, such as energy.  For instance, if a resource had 

an energy locational marginal price of $35/MWh and marginal energy cost of $30/MWh, the 

opportunity cost for ramp capability-up would be $5/MWh.  This means that the price for ramp 

capability-up will need to be at least $5/MWh for this resource to clear the product.  However, 

all dispatchable resource types with available ramp may participate in clearing this product, and 

congestion is not considered in the clearing.  This means that a resource behind a constraint 

may have a -$65/MWh energy locational marginal price and marginal energy cost of $10/MWh.  

In this case, this resources opportunity cost would be -$55/MWh.  The market-clearing engine 

considers all negative opportunity cost to have $0/MWh opportunity costs.  

There is a disconnect with the assumptions used in the clearing of the ramp capability product 

and the actual deployment of the ramp.  When the market clearing engine goes to deploy the 

ramp cleared behind the constraint, it is often too expensive to meet the ramp needs in the 

market.  This can make the market go short of products, when the cost of congestion relief is 

higher than the scarcity demand curves.  In the example above, the -$55/MWh opportunity cost 

will likely get more negative as the resource gets dispatched up for ramp.  When the costs to 

ramp-up the resource get higher than the scarcity or violation relaxation limits the market will 

go scarce the product and price it with the associated scarcity price.  Ramp capability scarcity 

demand curves are discussed in Section 3.2.3.  

Figure 3–31 below shows the 2023 megawatts of ramp capability-up cleared on resources with 

shift factors on a binding or breached constraint in the real-time market.   

State of the Market 2023 

104 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Figure 3–31  Real-time cleared ramp-up effect on constraints 

The above graph illustrates that there is a large percentage of ramp capability-up being cleared 

behind a binding or breached constraint in the real-time market.  The negative values represent 

resources with shift factors that increase congestion on a constraint, thus decreasing the ability 

to deliver ramp-up to the market.  This is a leading contributor to the market not being able to 

use the ramp procured.  SPP is working on a potential solution to reduce the effects of stranding 

ramp behind constraints.  

3.2.4.2.2   Ramp product demand curve prices are likely too low 

The ramp product prices scarcity with a demand curve.  The MMU had concerns that the 

demand curve prices for ramp are likely too low.  Prior to implementation, there was a concern 

that low prices may not allow the market to provision rampable capacity even though it is 

available.  Consequently, physical ramp may be insufficient, and the price may not reflect the 

actual value of ramp, which undermines the purpose of a ramp product.   

The MMU recommended that the maximum demand curve price be set slightly below the 

minimum regulation demand curve price.  Avoiding regulation scarcity events in the future is the 

primary goal of a ramp product.  A higher scarcity price may be needed to clear physical ramp 

and to incentivize ramp capability.  The MMU reviewed the ramp-up and ramp-down scarcity 

demand curve’s effectiveness.  Ramp-down has had a $0/MW market clearing price since 

implementation in March 2022.  This is due to the abundance of rampable down capacity 

available in the market.  

As stated before, ramp capability-up did have scarcity in both day-ahead and real-time markets.  

However, the MMU was unable to analyze the effects of the ramp-up demand curves, due to the 

State of the Market 2023 

105 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

issues discussed in the previous section concerning the deliverability of the procured ramp 

capability-up.  In order to assess the effectiveness of the demand curves, we recommend that 

SPP staff evaluate the effectiveness of the demand curves when assessing solutions to the 

stranded ramping issue.  There were no concerns with ramp capability-down scarcity demand 
curves as the product has never gone scarce in either the day-ahead or real-time markets.68  

3.2.4.2.3   Reduced need for instantaneous load capacity 

The process known as instantaneous load capacity is ramp procurement without ramp payment.  

The instantaneous load capacity ensures that sufficient rampable capacity is committed to ramp 

from one average hourly load to the next.  Resources committed to provide this rampable 

capacity add value to the market but are not paid for that value.  These resources often run at a 

financial loss for most of the hour and are merely made whole to their costs.  The instantaneous 

load capacity requirement is not removed or reduced by the proposed ramp product.  If more 
than one ramping timeframe is needed for more than one ramping purpose,69 then the market 

monitor could support multiple ramp products. 

SPP implemented an uncertainty product in July 2023 that works similarly to the ramp product, 
with a one-hour time horizon, and off-line resources are able to participate.70  The MMU 

believes that this product, in conjunction with the ramp capability product, should significantly 

reduce the need for the use of committing the uncompensated capacity, currently defined as 

instantaneous load capacity. 

  UNCERTAINTY RESERVE PRODUCT 

SPP implemented an uncertainty product on July 6, 2023.  The product’s objective is similar to 

the ramp product in that it was designed to reorganize the market dispatch to efficiently ensure 

rampable capacity for future needs, while effectively pricing and paying for the procured 

rampable capacity.  There are two main differences between the uncertianty product and the 

ramp product, which was implemented in 2022.  First, the ramp product has a 10 minute 

assessment horizon while the uncertainty product has a one hour horizon, for which they can 

both procure ramp.  Second, the uncertainty product allows clearing from offline and online 

resources; while the ramp product only clears from online resources.  

68 See Figures 3-13 and 3-14. 
69 For instance, it may be appropriate to have a ramp product similar to instantaneous load capacity to 
address inter-hour ramping needs in addition to an intra-hour ramp product to address short-term load 
variability.   
70 See RR449, FERC Docket No. ER22-914.   

State of the Market 2023 

106 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

The requirement for the uncertainty product has both a planned component and an uncertainty 

component.  The planned component is based on the hourly forecasted net load change 

between each hour.  The uncertainty component is based on historical net load forecast errors 
over the prior twelve months for a given hour.  The 97th percentile of the forecast errors is used 

to set the hourly uncertainty component.  Next, the uncertainty component and the planned 

component are combined to comprise the flexibility component of the ultimate uncertainty 

requirement.  In the last step of calculating the uncertainty requirement, regulation up, 

contengency reserve, and ramp product requirements are subtracted from the flexibility 

requirements.  The uncertainty reserve requirement will ultimately be the greater of the 
calculation and 0 MW.71 

Online resource offers are based on their opportunity cost between the locational marginal price 

and the resource’s variable energy offer, similar to the ramp product.  Soon-to-start resources 

are considered to have no opportunity costs, so their uncertainty offers are considered to be 
$0/MWh. 72  Offline offers are submitted by the market participant.  However, resources that 

clear real-time offline uncertainty and receive a market commitment during the same 

uncertainty horizon forgo cost reimbursement for start-up, no-load, and energy at minimum 

operating limit for the duration of their minimum run-time.  This was necessary to incentivize 

the generator owners to submit energy offers that include all the incremental costs the market 

must bear from committing and using the offline ramp associted with the offline uncertainty.  

Otherwise, having no opportunity cost, offline resources would have no incentive to offer 

anything more than $0/MWh, and the incremental cost to the market for the commitment 

would be largely borne in uplift rather than market prices.  

Prices for the uncertainty product have been low.  Figure 3–32, below, illustrates the count of 

hours each price point was used for setting the 2023 uncertainty market clearing prices (MCP) 

for both day-ahead and real-time uncertainty. 

71  Integrated Marketplace Ramp and Uncertainty Product Calculation Guide 
72  Soon-to-start resources are scheduled to synchronize to the grid within the uncertainty horizon.  It 
represents the resources’ collective megawatt output achievable by the end of the uncertainty horizon.  
For example, if a resource has a 30-minute startup to minimum time, a minimum limit of 30 megawatts, 
and is scheduled to start 45 minutes into the uncertainty horizon, then 15 megawatts are eligible for 
soon-to-start.  The residual 15 megawatts of rampable capacity can be used as soon-to-start in the next 
uncertainty horizon.

When the uncertainty price is $0/MWh, the uncetainty product is not creating any rampable 

capacity.  This is because the market clearing engine assumes there is plenty of rampable 

capacity available and there is no need to redispatch the market to create ramp.  The reasons for 

the low pricing can directly be attributed to the low requirements, and the clearing of soon-to-

start resources, both of which are disscussed below. 

As discussed above, the uncertianty requirement is set each hour by adding the planned and 

uncertainty component and then subtracting the contingency reserve, regulation-up, and ramp 

up requirements.  The megawatts associated with the operating reserve products are often 

greater than the planned and uncertainty components of the uncertainty requirement.  

Subtracting other product requirements from the uncertainty requirement frequently results in 

the uncertainty requirement being 0 MW.  Figure 3–33, below, illustrates the 2023 hourly

In just over 10 percent of the hours in 2023 where the uncertainty requirement was reduced by 

the other products’ ancillary service requirements, one of the other products went scarce.  In 

addition to this, there were multiple days where manual commitments were made by the SPP 

balancing authority for rampable capacity while the uncertainty requirement remained 0 MW.  

These two facts seem to indicate that fully relying on the rampable capacity from other ancillary 

service products to fill the uncertainty requirement is not effective.  

In addition to issues with the requirement frequently not getting set, the market is rarely pricing 

the uncertainty when there is a requirement.  As stated above, there was only one five-minute 

interval priced above $0/MWh in the real-time market, and only one percent of the hours had a 

price above $0/MWh in the day-ahead market.  One key reason for this is the clearing of 

uncertainty from soon-to-start resources.   

Resources that have commitments starting during the uncertainty horizon will be eligible to help 

fullfil the uncertainty requirement.  For instance, if a resource is scheduled to come on halfway 

into the hour and is expected to reach 50 MW of output over the remaining thirty minutes of 

the one hour uncertainty horizon, the resource will have 50 MW of expected rampable capacity 

available to help meet the uncertainty requirement.  However, it is important to note that soon-

to-start resources are not compensated for the uncertainty.  Also, soon-to-start resources are 

assumed to have no opportunity costs, so their offered cost for uncertainty is considered 

$0/MWh.  The two pie charts, shown below in Figure 3–35, illustrate the percentages of day-

ahead and real-time uncertainty megawatts cleared by soon-to-start, online, and offline 

resources.

requirement removes the need for the market to create ramp through redispatch, as soon-to-

start’s ramp is considered free to the market.

As stated above, resources that clear real-time uncertainty are not eligible for cost 

reimbursments for their start-up, no-load, and energy to minimum limits when committed 

within one hour after clearing real-time offline uncertianty.  Due to this, the markets have seen 

much higher real-time offers than day-ahead offers for uncertainty.  We will discuss the 

settlement implications of this in the next section.

The disparity in day-ahead and real-time prices has caused an arbitrage opportunity for offline 

resources.  Offline uncertainty resources only forego make-whole-payment cost reimbursements 

when clearing offline uncertainty in the real-time market while getting committed within the 

uncertainty horizon.  This provision is not there when clearing offline uncertainty in the day-

ahead market.  Consequently, several resources are offering in $0/MWh uncertainty offers in the 

day-ahead market then raising their offers in real-time as to not clear real-time offline 

uncertainty.  Because the real-time market-clearing price is almost always $0/MWh, there is little

risk in buy back cost.  This means the generator owners get to keep the payments they received

reductions.

The market rules that led to deliverability issues in the ramp product are present with the 

uncertainty product.  However, the low volume of clearing seems to mask some of the issue with 

the uncertainty product.  In addition, the longer clearing horizon may also be helping clear more 

deliverable capacity.  In any case, the majority of online uncertainty cleared seems to be coming 

from generation relieving constraints.  The market monitor will keep monitoring the uncertainty 

product for deliverability to ensure this pattern does not change.  

There are several key issues with the current design of the uncertainty product.  The market 

rarely prices the product, meaning very little ramp is created through redispatch.  In fact, the 

real-time price was only set above $0/MWh for one interval, and the day-ahead was set above 

$0/MWh less than one percent of the time in 2023.  When the price of the product is $0/MWh, it 

has no effect on the market, as no resources are redispatched, no rampable capacity is created, 

and no resources are compensated for the ramp provided.  

There are two main drivers for the $0/MWh prices.  First, the backing out of other ancillary 

service requirements from the uncertainty product’s requirement, leading to an uncertainty 

product requirement to 0 MW during 82 percent of hours in 2023.  Second, the clearing of 

uncertainty product by soon-to-start resources also drives down the price of uncertainty, as 

State of the Market 2023 

113 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

soon-to-start resources are not paid for the rampable capacity they provide, and they cannot set 

price. 

The deliverability issues seen with the ramp product has not materialized with the uncertainty 

product.  However, the uncertainty product has all of the same attributes that lead to the 

deliverability problem with the ramp product.  It appears that the low volumes of online 

uncertainty cleared in the real-time market is likely masking the issue.   

The infrequency of real-time prices above $0/MWh in the real-time market has created an 

arbitrage opportunity for offline resources.  Some of the resources are adjusting their offline 

uncertainty offers between day-ahead and real-time to take advantage of day-ahead uncertainty 

prices and avoid possible make whole payment reductions in real-time.  

The MMU has two recommendations for SPP surrounding the uncertainty product: 

1)  SPP should review the requirement settings for the uncertainty product and make 

corrections to ensure adequate rampable capacity is procured through the product.  In 

particular, the MMU recommends that SPP look at the practice of reducing the 

uncertainty requirement with the other products’ requirements.  

2)  SPP should review the use of soon-to-start resources in meeting the requirement for 

uncertainty.  Soon-to-start resources are not counted in any other way when optimizing 

other market products such as energy or ancillary services. 

3.3  RESOURCE AVAILABILITY 

Resource availability represents the generating capacity available to the market to serve load 

and ancillary service requirements.  Available capacity has been a growing concern for SPP.  

While the region used to have a large excess of generating capacity, the margin between 

available capacity and load has been shrinking as conventional resources retire and load grows.  

A greater proportion of SPP’s resources are variable energy resources that are not fully within 

SPP’s control.  Maintaining reliability requires SPP to have a reasonable estimate of the capacity 

available for dispatch at any given time, particularly during resource adequacy seasons when 

tight conditions are most likely to occur.  The supply adequacy process currently only applies to 

the summer resource adequacy season.  The main tool SPP employs to ensure there is adequate 

generation the rest of the year is the outage coordination process.  Resources cannot run 

constantly at full capacity and occasionally need to be out of service or derated.  When a 

resource is out of service, its entire capacity is unavailable for dispatch.  When a resource is 

derated, a portion of the capacity of the resource is unavailable for dispatch.  The outage 

State of the Market 2023 

114 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

coordination process ensures that the amount of capacity on outage at a given time will not 

pose a threat to reliability. 

This section reviews general resource availability compared to the region’s load obligation.  It 

also reviews SPP’s accreditation and outage coordination processes and assesses their 

effectiveness at ensuring sufficient capacity is available to SPP for dispatch.  

  AVAILABLE CAPACITY 

In 2023, SPP’s nameplate capacity increased by 1.3 GW to just over 100 GW while accredited 

capacity dropped slightly from 64.5 GW to 63.4 GW.  Average hourly available capacity dropped 

from 63.7 GW to 56.6 GW.  Figure 3–39 and Figure 3–40 depict how, relative to previous years, 

2023 saw a marked decrease in availability needed to meet load and reserve requirements.  This 

has largely been driven by the retirement and underperformance of conventional resources 

combined with an increased reliance on variable energy resources and imports from 

neighboring RTOs. 

Looking at generation availability relative to demand shows the reliability margin available to 

the region.  Figure 3–39 shows, for each day, the minimum hourly capacity available from all 

internal resources registered in the market as a percentage of load plus contingency reserve for 

that same hour for 2021, 2022, and 2023.

available capacity and demand varies significantly.  During 2023, minimum availability as a
