minimum availability was 104 percent during late summer.    

As the proportion of variable energy resources (VERs) in SPP’s supply mix continues to grow, the 

system’s need to count on the availability of conventional resources increases.  In 2023, 

conventional resources accounted for 80 percent of the available generation on average, 

ranging from 60 percent to nearly 100 percent.  Variable energy resources, primarily wind, 

accounted for 20 percent on average, ranging from less than one percent to nearly 40 percent.  

Figure 3–40 compares internal conventional capacity to its actual availability and load plus 

contingency reserves by hour for 2023.  The hours when total conventional capacity is nearly 

equal to load plus contingency reserves are often those where system reliability depended on 

variable energy resources.  Because the output of these resources are variable and forecast 

based, frequent reliance on variable energy resources to prevent operating reserve or energy 

shortages without adequate improvements to transmission and investment in energy storage or 

complementary conventional generation represents a real risk to system reliability.

SPP enforces a resource adequacy requirement (RAR) during the summer season. 74   As stated 

above, the resource adequacy requirement requires all load responsible entities to accredit 

enough capacity to cover their net peak load plus the planning reserve margin.  For the resource 

adequacy requirement to be effective in ensuring reliability, the MMU recommends the 

accreditation process render an accredited capacity that reflects true generation availability 

when it is most needed.  For resources that are not covered under SPP’s proposed effective load 
carrying capability (ELCC) process,75 the current capacity accreditation process for ensuring 

available capacity to serve demand is determined based on a capability test performed every 

five years and a one hour operational test annually to demonstrate a resource is able to perform 

at least 90 percent of its capability test.  However, this does not typically reflect a meaningful 

expectation of how much SPP may be able to rely on capacity when it is needed most.  

Recognizing this, SPP and stakeholders filed a new performance based accreditation 

methodology along with an updated effective load carrying capability methodology with FERC 

in late February 2024.

filing.  SPP is currently working on an updated revision.

resources.  See ER24-1317.

In 2023, the internal accredited capacity was approximately 63,376 MW, essentially all total 

accredited capacity in SPP.  Resources not using effective load carrying capability accreditation 

(steam turbines, large hydro, etc.) accounted for 57,573 MW or 91 percent of accredited 

capacity.  Figure 3–43  shows that, for the non-effective load carrying capability accredited 

megawatts, roughly 78 percent were available on average in 2023.  That figure went as high as 

over 90 percent and as low as almost 60 percent.  Figure 3-48 also shows how availability 

dramatically dropped during winter events in 2021 and 2022.  These figures are notably lower 

than the estimated performance-based accreditation in both summer and winter.

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Currently, the tariff lacks appropriate incentives for resources to remain available during the 

resource adequacy season as there is no mechanism that addresses units that were claimed as 

capacity but become unavailable after February 15.  Load responsible entities who have capacity 

that is accredited but is unavailable for the entire summer will not be subject to the capacity 

deficiency payment, the main tariff mechanism incentivizing load responsible entities to carry an 

appropriate level of capacity.  Without a mechanism to incentivize or ensure availability, there is 

a disconnect between the capacity SPP accredits for resource adequacy and the actual capacity 

available to operate the system reliably.  

Additionally, the resource adequacy requirement omits capacity shortages in non-summer 
months as there is currently no resource adequacy requirement for winter, spring, or fall.77  

Capacity shortages have been seen across every season, potentially compromising system 

reliability.  For instance, capacity shortages can occur during extreme winter weather, such as the 

February 2021 winter weather event, the December 2022 winter storm, and the winter event in 

January 2024.  In all cases, a large portion of capacity accredited for the summer season was not 

available due to system shocks such as fuel deliverability and quality issues, equipment failures, 

and icing.  Planning for peak summer days as the only shock to capacity availability lacks the 

necessary complexity to ensure reliability year-round. 

While there is no way to be certain that capacity will be available in real time, incentives can 

provide much more certainty than exists with today’s steel-in-the-ground capacity requirement.  

SPP and its stakeholders should continue to work on mechanisms that reward resources that 

perform more reliably and are available when needed by SPP operations.  The MMU also 

continues to recommend that a resource adequacy requirement be applied to all four seasons.  

The significant troughs in actual availability in the spring and autumn seasons reflect a lower 

forecasted demand and lower probability of a significant event.  This should be reflected in a 

lower supply adequacy requirement that would provide SPP a reasonable estimate of actual 

expected available generation while giving resources an opportunity to take planned 

maintenance outages when the risks and needs to the system change.  

Overall, the MMU has the same four critical recommendations related to supply adequacy, with 

slight modifications based on lessons learned over the past three years: 

1)  If SPP is to rely on any resource to provide energy, then that resource should be 

available.  The resource adequacy process should consider all outages to represent 

expected availability more accurately.  It should also include an analysis of outage 

77 See footnote 34. 

State of the Market 2023 

120 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

correlation between resources in similar areas using the same fuels.  Deliverability should 

also be considered, like the effective load carrying capability methodology.  

2)  There should be meaningful incentives related to reliability.  There should be market, 

out-of-market, and/or regulatory policy mechanisms to incentivize reliability attributes.  

These attributes include but are not limited to flexibility on outage timing and duration, 

dual-fuel capability, and winterization. 

3)  A more frequent resource adequacy requirement, such as a seasonal (or perhaps 

monthly) requirement, should be developed.  The February 2021 winter weather event 

and December 2022 winter storm both demonstrated the importance of having an 

accurate expectation of resource availability during cold weather, not just summer peak. 

Our data also demonstrate low availability levels during the shoulder seasons when most 

planned outages are taken.  The MMU recommends exploring the feasibility of a four-

season resource adequacy requirement.  

4)  SPP should plan for shocks to generator availability including adverse weather events, 

pipeline outages, wind turbine icing, and solar eclipses.  Express attention should be paid 

to the pattern and correlation of outages between resource types and locations.  SPP 

should implement mitigation measures to ensure the reliable operation of the grid under 

all circumstances.  This may include strategic investments in transmission to enhance 

deliverability of capacity, development of energy storage resources, and price sensitive 

demand. 

  RESOURCE OUTAGES 

Resource outages are the main contributor to resource unavailability for both accredited and 

unaccredited capacity.  Two major reasons for resource outages are generator maintenance and 

a forced event, such as an equipment failure.  Generally, maintenance outages are planned or 

scheduled in advance to perform routine work.  As the reliability coordinator and balancing 

authority, SPP determines a maintenance margin, which is the amount of capacity allocated for 

planned and opportunity generator outages at a given time.  SPP approves, denies, or 

reschedules these outages to ensure system reliability using the maintenance margin and 
following the outage coordination methodology.78   

Forced outages, by contrast, are generally unpredictable, resulting from sudden equipment 

failure, fuel supply disruptions, or extreme events such as icing or flooding.  However, the MMU 

78 SPP Reliability Coordinator Outage Coordination Methodology.  

State of the Market 2023 

121 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

has also seen instances where market participants put a resource on forced outage for economic 

reasons, such as not wanting to staff the resource or to take an outage that was not approved 

by SPP.  Forced outages are not known or studied in advance of the outage by SPP operations 

and may pose a reliability risk, especially during periods of capacity scarcity.  As such, 

minimizing the frequency and duration of forced outages is a critical piece of ensuring the 

reliability operation of the grid.  This requires creating the right incentives for resources to 

mitigate exposure to potential shocks such as freezing and penalizing resources that use forced 

outages inappropriately. 

The amount of capacity on outage has risen over time.  2021 saw a large spike in outages due to 

maintenance that was deferred during the height of the COVID-19 pandemic.  The dip in 

outages that followed in 2022 was not indicative of a decline in outaged capacity, but a return to 

pre-pandemic outage capacity levels.   

Overall, from 2022 to 2023, there was an increase in total outaged capacity of over six percent – 

nearing levels similar to that of 2021.  More specifically, both maintenance outages and forced 

outages increased by six percent.  This can be seen in Figure 3–44, which shows capacity derated 
and taken out-of-service by reason—forced or maintenance.79  Each reason is further 

categorized by technology type.

As in previous years, capacity taken out-of-service for maintenance accounts for the largest 

portion of outaged capacity.  This is followed by capacity forced out-of-service, forced derates, 

and finally, by maintenance derates respectively.  Coal provided about 22 percent of total 
generation80 and accounted for about 30 percent of outaged capacity in 2023.  Combined cycle 

gas provided nearly 14 percent of total generation and about 19 percent of outaged capacity, a 

similar ratio to coal.  However, simple-cycle gas provided nearly 23 percent of total generation, 

but about 34 percent of outaged capacity, a higher ratio than coal or combined-cycle gas.  

Inversely, wind provided nearly 34 percent of total generation and only about 11 percent of 

outaged capacity, making it the lowest ratio of the various technology types.  

Outages and derates lasting longer than seven days are considered long-term.  The majority of 

long-term outaged capacity is for planned and maintenance.  While these outages have 

increased by 12 percent since 2021, these outages are studied by SPP to ensure they do not 

pose an undue risk to reliability.  Forced outages are a growing proportion of long-term 

outages, increasing from roughly 39 percent to 44 percent of long-term outages between 2021 

and 2023.  Overall, long-term forced outages have increased by 36 percent since 2021.  This is a 

concerning trend as forced outages potentially pose a greater reliability risk. 

Overall, long-term outaged capacity increased by over seven percent from 2022 to 2023.

accredited capacity.

  Correlated outages 

Correlated output, including correlated outages, is a growing area of concern in SPP.  In 2018, 

SPP and stakeholders recognized that maintaining reliability as the region sees a large increase 

in variable energy resources, whose output is highly correlated, requires a new approach to 

accreditation for these resources.  Following the industry trend, SPP filed a tariff revision with 

FERC in February 2024 that would adopt an effective load carrying capability methodology for 

accreditation of variable energy resources to explicitly account for the reliability risk associated 

with their output that is both highly correlated and not fully controllable.  However, variable 

energy resources are not the only resources that pose reliability risks due to correlated output.  

Examining data since 2021 provides evidence that forced outages among conventional 

resources tend to be highly correlated as well.  This makes sense as resources that share similar 

fuel supply chains or have components susceptible to the same shocks such as icing will likely 

experience outages at similar times or in response to the same event, such as a winter storm.   

Figure 3–47 and Figure 3–48 show the distribution of the percentage of capacity on forced 

outage at by fuel type during the summer season and proposed winter season, respectively.  

While percentage of fleet is a good measure of correlation between outages, the reliability 

impact of these correlated outages also depends on how many resources are on outage and the 

amount of capacity on outage.  If a small percentage of coal resources is on outage but this 

represents a large amount of capacity, this could present a greater reliability issue than a larger 

percentage of oil units on outage that represents a small amount of total capacity.  Thus, the call 

outs identify the median amount of capacity and number of units on forced outage by fuel type.

The correlation and relative reliability impact of forced outages by fuel type is about the same 

for both seasons.  Wind and hydro resources have a consistent portion of their fleet on outage, 

with a median of two percent and three percent on forced outage, respectively, for summer.  For 

winter, the medians are one percent and four percent on forced outage, respectively.  Coal 

resources have the highest median percent of capacity on forced outage at 15 percent in 

summer and 13 percent in winter; however, the amount of capacity on outage is normally 

distributed and clustered tightly around the median.  This translates into more than a dozen coal 

resources out at time, representing 2,800 to 3,300 megawatts on outage.   

State of the Market 2023 

126 

 
 
  
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Natural gas and wind resources tend to have the largest number of resources on forced outage 

at the same time.  For natural gas, this represents 2,800 to 3,500 megawatts on outage.  

Additionally, natural gas has more outlier events with extreme amounts of capacity (as much as 

16,000 megawatts) on forced outage simultaneously.  Wind forced outages generally account 

for 1,100 to 1,400 megawatts of capacity.  Like natural gas, wind experiences more outlier 

events; however, simultaneous wind forced outages also tend to occur more frequently at the 

lowest ranges of total capacity.   

The differences in capacity on outage versus total units demonstrates how wind resources tend 

to have smaller installed capacities, natural gas resources are slightly larger, and coal resources 

are, on average, the largest resources.  Each pose different challenges for reliability and reserves.  

Having a smaller number of resources on simultaneous forced outage versus a larger number 

with the same capacity does not matter from a capacity and reserves standpoint.  In general, it is 

more common and likely that two larger coal resources go on forced outage at the same time 

than many natural gas resources totaling the same capacity.  However, from a reliability 

standpoint, having a larger number of resources go on forced outage simultaneously can pose a 

greater challenge.  This is due to the impact of multiple resources’ output (or lack thereof) on 

congestion.  The more flowgates impacted by forced outages, the more SPP must manage for 

congestion.   

Correlated forced outages, whether posing a challenge for reserves, managing congestion, 

reliability, or a combination of the three, should be central to the supply adequacy process.  

While the proposed ELCC methodology for accreditation explicitly considers correlated output, 

the proposed PBA methodology for accreditation for conventional resources does not.  The data 

above suggests that correlated outages among conventional resources do have a significant 

impact to availability and hence, should be considered in the accreditation process.   

In addition to identifying correlated outages, having the ability to understand the causes allows 

for a better prediction of forced outages and to take steps to mitigate their causes.  Currently, 

outage data are often incomplete or insufficient to determine driving causes behind forced 

outages.  More accurate, complete, and standardized outage data would allow SPP and the 

MMU to identify common causes of forced outages. 

  Fuel assurance 

A frequent cause of forced outages, and often correlated forced outages, is fuel procurement.  

This can include supply chain issues, deliverability issues, quality issues, and financial/credit risk 

issues, particularly related to the misalignment of the gas and electric markets.  

State of the Market 2023 

127 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Although SPP and market participants adjusted the day-ahead market commitment schedule 

through the stakeholder process, natural gas timely nominations are still due prior to market 
participants receiving their commitment schedule.81  Depending on the pipeline’s capacity and 

the type of service the gas generator has, this could have multiple undesired consequences 

including market participants not procuring fuel without a day-ahead commitment then not 

being able to procure gas economically (or at all) in real-time or procuring natural gas without a 

day-head commitment then being charged parking or other fees when the gas is not used.  

Several wholesale natural gas and electricity organizations, including SPP, have been exploring 

new market rules and policies that are intended to incentivize procurement of the most reliable 

lowest cost natural gas service, firm timely natural gas service, when appropriate, as well as 

investment in secondary and/or stored fuel sources for capacity resources.  In response to the 
February 2021 winter weather event, the North American Energy Standards Board, or NAESB,82 

initiated a second Gas-Electric Harmonization (GEH) forum that began in August 2022 and 

culminated in a final recommendation report submitted to the FERC chair and NERC president 
on July 28, 2023.83  The report identified 20 recommendations that were intended to improve 

the reliability of natural gas-fired electric fleet as well as the upstream supply of natural gas 

during extreme winter weather events.  Most of the recommendations did not receive support 

across both electric and gas industry stakeholders.  Further, on May 3, 2023, SPP along with 
broad coalition of key bulk electric system operators and stakeholders,84 filed a standards 

development request to implement Recommendation 4 from the report which suggested 

removing extreme winter weather from the force-majeure clause of the NAESB base gas 

contract.  During the subsequent kick-off meeting for the wholesale gas quadrant, gas 

producers unanimously urged dismissal of the request, effectively terminating further discussion 

of the issue.  Due to the discord across the gas and electricity sectors, the GEH final report 

suggested federal legislative action would be needed to create a reliability-based regulatory 

body for the natural gas industry to get natural gas stakeholders to implement the Report’s 

recommendations.  In October 2023, a coalition of North American market monitors, led by the 

81 Docket No. ER19-2681. 
82 NAESB serves as an industry forum for the development and promotion of standards which help develop 
a seamless marketplace for wholesale and retail natural gas and electricity, as recognized by its customers, 
business community, participants, and regulatory entities. 
83 https://www.naesb.org/pdf4/geh_final_report_072823.pdf 
84 The coalition includes PJM Interconnection, MISO Energy, Texas Competitive Power Association, UGI 
Utilities and CenterPoint Energy. 

State of the Market 2023 

128 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

SPP MMU, submitted joint comments to FERC and NERC in general support of the GEH’s efforts 
and recommendations.85  

In November 2023, the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners (NARUC), 

announced a new 15-month Gas-Electric Alignment for Reliability (GEAR) initiative which brings 

together stakeholders representing both state and industry interests to help identify solutions to 

improve the reliability of both the wholesale gas and electric industries.  The final set of 

recommendations is set to be announced at NARUC’s February 2025 winter policy summit. 

Finally, SPP and its stakeholders have been working to develop several market revisions 

intended to improve gas-electric coordination and encourage generators to invest in fuel 
assurance to help meet SPP’s resource adequacy requirements.  Revision Request 61286 

introduces a multi-day economic commitment process that, in theory, should signal resources to 

procure gas ahead of time and at the most economic rate possible while protecting them from 
some fuel uncertainty risk.  Revision Request 62187 modifies the proposed conventional resource 

accreditation practices to limit the accredited megawatts for resources that have been on full or 

partial forced outages due to fuel constraints during hours in which they were most needed.  In 

theory, this approach would create incentives for generators to invest in infrastructure or 

contracts which would either firm their respective natural gas supplies or allow the units to 

operate with a secondary fuel source located on-site.  SPP staff intends both revision requests to 

be filed with FERC towards the end of 2024 following thorough stakeholder discussions and 

voting. 

  Outage coordination 

To balance the need to perform routine and preventative maintenance and to maintain grid 

reliability, SPP uses a centralized outage coordination process.  This process includes setting a 

maintenance margin, which is the amount of capacity that can safely be on planned or 

maintenance outage based on expected grid conditions at a given time.  Numerous factors, 

including an aging fleet, generator retirements, and an increase in variable energy resource 

penetration have led to a narrowing maintenance margin over time.  This means to ensure 

outages are efficiently prioritized and approved, the accuracy and transparency of the outage 

scheduling process is ever more important.   

85 https://www.nerc.com/news/Headlines%20DL/Inter_RTO_Monitor_GEH_Letter_NERC[52].pdf 
86 https://www.spp.org/spp-documents-filings/?id=21069; “RR612 Multi-Day Economic Commitment” 
87 https://www.spp.org/spp-documents-filings/?id=21069; “RR621 Fuel Assurance”

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Contributing to the efficiency of the outage coordination process is outage submittal data.  The 

outage coordination methodology requires a reason and planned end date for the outage at 
both the time of the outage submittal and at the submittal of each change.88  SPP uses these 

data to study each outage to determine the priority of the outage, if the outage as submitted 

will pose any reliability risks, and to ensure the total amount of capacity on outage does not 

exceed the maintenance margin during the proposed timeframe.  Because outage submissions 

are intended to be used for assessing real-time and future reliability of the bulk electric 
system,89 it is critical that the outage information submitted to SPP is accurate.  

The MMU has observed insufficient, omitted, delayed, and incorrect outage information.  The 

MMU maintains material misstatements of outage information could be considered providing 
false information to the RTO and may result in referral to FERC.90  However, these data are hard 

if not impossible to validate after the fact.  Instead, greater care should be taken on the front 

end to ensure that outages are not studied or approved until all necessary data are submitted 

and validated for accuracy.  

Another area of concern is outage extensions.  An outage’s original scope of work cannot always 

be completed in the original scheduled timeframe.  Therefore, the current outage coordination 

methodology allows an outage to be extended.  However, outage extensions can reduce 

efficiency of the outage coordination process.  Regardless of the outage extension priority, 

outage extensions are given priority for approval, similar to forced outages.  Therefore, outage 

extensions have the potential to be inappropriately prioritized higher than new outages, 

resulting in denial of the new outages.  In addition, outage extensions decrease the amount of 

available maintenance margin and, due to their unexpected nature, can adversely affect other 

processes such as the congestion hedging market.   

Figure 3–49 shows generator outage extensions that are greater than one week by duration. 

88 A forced outage can be submitted with an unknown cause, but the cause and planned end date are 
required to be updated promptly as soon as more information is known. 
89 SPP Reliability Coordinator Outage Coordination Methodology, Section 1 
90 18 CFR § 35.41

In 2023, there were 813 outages extended longer than one week, a two percent decrease from 

2022.  The MMU encourages SPP to implement processes and mechanisms to properly 

schedule, classify, report, and prioritize outage extensions.  

The MMU remains concerned about the improper use of outage extensions, especially outage 

extensions of long duration.  The MMU has observed outages extended for reasons different 

than the original outage reason, resulting in misclassified and nontransparent outage 

extensions.  Misclassified outages can affect out-of-market budgetary items, such as state 

recovery rates and taxes.  For some performance-based resource adequacy calculations, 

misclassified outages could affect the amount of capacity accredited under SPP’s stakeholder 
approved performance-based accreditation approach.91   

Even though there are legitimate reasons for resources to be on outage, proper incentives and 

procedures can promote efficiency, reliability, and proper outage behavior.  To these ends, the 

MMU recommends SPP: 

•  Bolster the outage coordination methodology by requiring all necessary supporting 

information be submitted before an outage can be submitted for review and approval.  

91 Such as the Generator Testing Task Force’s recommendation of EFOF’.  See "GTTF Performance 
Accreditation Educational Overview_Final.pdf”, available at 
https://www.spp.org/Documents/66148/SAWG%20Agenda%20and%20Background%20Materials%202021
1208.zip.

Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 3 
Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

In addition, SPP should set in place a process for verifying and validating the submitted 

data to ensure the proper outage priority and status are assigned.   

•  Validate that an outage extension is needed to finish the original outage’s scope of work.  

When this cannot be validated, SPP should treat the extension as a new outage request. 

•  Develop a clear link between the outage coordination and resource adequacy processes 

to ensure transparency and accountability.  This will not only improve the accuracy of the 

accreditation process but will provide a proper incentive for resources to not misclassify 

or misuse outages.

Market prices and costs 

4  MARKET PRICES AND COSTS 

This chapter covers market prices and costs in the SPP market, along with related metrics on 

negative prices, make-whole payments, and long-run price signals for investment.  Highlights of 

this chapter include:  

•  Average gas price for 2023 at the Panhandle Eastern hub was $2.17/MMBtu, down 63 

percent from $5.83/MMBtu in 2022.  Lower gas prices in 2023 are a key driver for many 

of the decreased prices and costs discussed in this section. 

•  The day-ahead market price for 2023 averaged $26/MWh, a decrease of 46 percent from 

$48/MWh in 2022, while the real-time market price averaged $24/MWh for 2023, down 

47 percent from $44/MWh in 2022.   

•  The frequency of negative price intervals decreased by 10 percent in the day-ahead 

market and by 19 percent in the real-time market from 2022.  Six percent of the day-

ahead asset owner intervals had negative prices in 2023, down from seven percent in 

2022.  In 2023, 12 percent of all asset owner intervals in the real-time market had 

negative prices, down from 15 percent in 2022.   

•  Day-ahead make-whole payments were nearly $79 million in 2023, down from $172 

million in 2022, a decrease of 54 percent.  Reliability unit commitment make-whole 

payments in 2023 totaled $157 million, down from $292 million in 2022, representing a 

47 percent decrease.  Much of the higher level of make-whole payments in 2022 can be 

attributed to higher gas prices during the year. 

• 

In 2023, 62 resources received over $1 million in total make-whole payments, compared 

to 160 in 2022, with the highest payment to a single resource at just over $10 million.  

On a market participant level, 10 market participants each received annual make-whole 

payments in excess of $10 million.  These 10 market participants accounted for 80 

percent of the total make-whole payments paid out in 2023. 

•  Total monthly revenue neutrality uplift for 2023 was $205 million, down 62 percent from 

$546 million in 2022.  The real-time joint operating agreement portion of revenue 

neutrality uplift was $94 million in 2023, a decrease of 43 percent from $165 million in 

2022.  The real-time congestion component of revenue neutrality uplift was $283 million 

in 2023, a decrease of 57 percent from $654 million in 2022.

State of the Market 2023 

133 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 4 
Market prices and costs 

•  Historically, market revenues alone have been insufficient to support the cost of new 

entry for all major generation technologies since the start of the Integrated Marketplace.  

Exceptions to this have been in 2021, due to increased revenue due to the February 

winter storm and in 2022 for certain wind resources.  In 2023, this broader trend largely 

continued.  However, combined-cycle and combustion turbine generation in the Western 

Area Power Administration, Upper Great Plains East region were able to recover their 

cost of new entry due to congestion-driven price spikes throughout the year. 

4.1  MARKET PRICES AND COSTS 

This section reviews market prices and costs by focusing on the energy market and fuel prices, 

price volatility, negative prices, operating reserve prices, and market settlement results.  Overall, 

annual energy prices were down from previous years in both the day-ahead and real-time 

markets.  This decrease can be mostly attributed to one key factor – the decrease in fuel prices, 

primarily gas, but also coal.

Historically, on-peak prices tend to average about $15/MWh higher than off-peak prices, in both 

day-ahead and real-time.  However, in 2022, the average spread between on-peak and off-peak 

prices grew to a difference of $25/MWh.  The spread dropped back into the $15/MWh range in 

2023.  Summer months typically experience the largest spread in both the day-ahead and real-

time, approaching $25/MWh in 2023.  The differences between on-peak and off-peak prices can 

be mostly attributed to lower loads and a higher percentage of lower cost wind generation in 

off-peak hours. 

Changes in gas prices have historically had the highest impact on electricity prices compared to 

other fuels.  This is because the short-run marginal costs of coal-fired generation historically 

have been cheaper than natural gas-fired generation.  The energy pricing continued to trend 

with the gas prices throughout the year.  Figure 4–4 compares various fuel price indices with

Often, natural gas is a byproduct of oil drilling.  Natural gas production has continued to 

outpace takeaway capacity in this area, with incremental production volumes quickly inundating 

any available space in the pipelines and keeping supply-area prices at discounts compared to 

other hubs.

2023, and the 2023 price for 8,800 Btu/lb. was $0.88/MMBtu, 21 cents below the 2022 average.

Typically, the SPP South hub prices exceed the SPP North hub prices.  After a spread in the 

$7/MWh range for 2021 (without February included) between the North and South hubs in both 

the day-ahead and real-time markets, the spread increased to $14/MWh in the day-ahead and 

$17/MWh in the real-time in 2022.  The spread decreased to $3/MWh in the day-ahead market 

and $2/MWh in the real-time market in 2023.  The increased separation in 2022 coincides with 

the increase in natural gas costs and congestion.  The general pattern of higher prices in the 

south and lower in the north is primarily due to fuel mix and congestion.  Coal, nuclear, and 

wind are the dominant fuels in the north and west.  The south and east have a much larger share 

of gas generation, hence, more exposure to changes in fuel prices. 

On a monthly basis, South hub prices typically exceed Norh hub prices, however, due to 

localized congestion patterns, North hub prices can exceed South hub prices, as was the case 

during a few months in 2023.

Average on-peak day-ahead prices dropped at both the North and South hubs of SPP, mostly

  ENERGY PRICE VOLATILITY  

Price volatility100 in the SPP market is shown in Figure 4–8 below.  As expected, day-ahead prices 

are much less volatile than those in real-time.  The day-ahead market does not experience the 

actual (unexpected) congestion and changes in load or generation found in the real-time 

market.  Real-time volatility tends to peak in the spring and fall, roughly corresponding with 

times of higher wind and lower load but can also peak during the summer months because of 

peak load conditions.

In 2023, volatility in both the day-ahead market and real-time market decreased.  Day-ahead

well, from 76 in 2022 to 58 in 2023.  Much of the decreased volatility can be attributed to 

decreased congestion and more stable fuel prices.

varying levels of congestion on the system, which is based on the layout of the transmission 

system and the distribution of the types of generation in the fleet.  The volatility for the majority 

of asset owners is consistent with the SPP average in both the day-ahead and real-time markets 

as shown in Figure 4–9.

Increased volatility was observed in certain geographic areas in 2023.  Areas in Texas and the far

  DAY-AHEAD AND REAL-TIME PRICE CONVERGENCE 

Price convergence between day-ahead and real-time prices is important, because the more day-

ahead prices reflect real-time prices, the better unit commitment and positioning of resources 

occurs for real-time operations.   

While average prices in the day-ahead and real-time markets have been close over the past 

several years, average prices can mask real-time volatility and underlying price differences.  The 

averaging of price spikes, and in particular, high prices during periods of scarcity, drove real-

time average prices up, closer to day-ahead prices.  These short-term, transient price spikes can 
be attributed to limitations in ramping capability.101   

In this section, underlying differences in prices after controlling for scarcity events are 

highlighted.  This analysis shows that a significant volume of generation, particularly from wind 

resources, not cleared in the day-ahead market, drives down real-time prices.   

101 For further information on ramping issues, see Section 3.2.4. 

State of the Market 2023 

142 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 4 
Market prices and costs 

Many factors cause prices to diverge between the day-ahead and real-time markets.  Some of 

these factors may include, but are not limited to: 

•  Day-ahead offers may include premiums to account for uncertainty in real-time fuel 

prices.102  

•  Load and wind forecast errors can cause differences in the real-time market results.  

•  Participants may not bid in all load or offer all generation in the day-ahead market.  

•  Modeling differences including transmission outages between the two markets.  

•  Generation outages or derates that were different in real-time than was anticipated in 

the day-ahead.  

• 

Impacts from other RTOs, that were not anticipated, may affect the SPP real-time market. 

•  Changes in imports and exports from other systems in the real-time markets. 

•  Unanticipated weather changes affect the real-time markets. 

Price divergence103 between the day-ahead and real-time markets at the system level is shown 

in Figure 4–10 below.  Market participants may be willing to pay a premium for more price 

certainty in in day-ahead market.  This can result in higher prices in the day-ahead market.  A 

large divergence between the day-ahead and real-time prices may also indicate that actual 

conditions in the market do not match expected conditions.  An extended period of a large 

variance between day-ahead and real-time prices may indicate a structural or design deficiency 

in the market. 

102 Additionally, Revision Request 239 allowed historic fuel cost uncertainty to be considered in the 
development of mitigated energy offers. 
103 Price divergence is calculated as the difference between day-ahead and real-time prices, using system 
prices for each five-minute (real-time) or hour (day-ahead) interval.  The absolute divergence is calculated 
by taking the absolute value of the divergence for each interval.

Average absolute divergence for 2023 was $12.69/MWh, down from $19.89/MWh in 2022.  

Historically, absolute divergence has averaged around $10 per month up through 2020.  The 

under-clearing of renewable resources and short-term ramping limitations contribute to 

increased price divergence.  It was hoped that implementation of the ramping product on March 

1, 2022 would also have a positive effect on this divergence; however, this has not been the case 

to date.  Moreover, the MMU has recommended many improvements resulting from the 

February winter weather event and recommends that additional work be done to improve price 
divergence related to under-clearing of renewable generation in the day-ahead market.104

of resources and virtual transactions in the day-ahead market and interchange transaction in the real-time 
balancing market to converge energy flows between the markets.

The marginal energy cost is one of three components that factor into locational marginal prices 

and represents the marginal cost to provide the next increment of dispatch absent losses and 

congestion.  Day-ahead prices are generally at a premium when compared to real-time prices 

(excluding scarcity pricing), particularly in the off-peak hours.  Also of note is that the marginal 

energy prices follow the same monthly trend as gas prices.

Wind generation made up 70 percent of the 3,938 MW of incremental real-time generation in

In 2023, 86 percent of the wind offered in the day-ahead cleared.  This is down from 88 percent 

in 2022 and 91 percent in 2021.  In 2023, 92 percent of forecasted wind was offered into the 

day-ahead, this is well below the 102 percent in 2022 and 101 percent in 2021.  However, even 

though wind resources are generally offering in close to full forecasted capacity to the day-

ahead, a portion of this is at offer levels that exceed prevailing prices and thus does not clear the 

market.  Typically, wind will clear all of the megawatts that are physically offered into the day-

ahead market if the economic offers are consistent with real-time offers.  The MMU observed 

that almost all wind megawatts offered into the day-ahead market and not cleared had higher 

economic offers in the day-ahead market than real-time. 

Systematic under-clearing of wind resources in the day-ahead market can contribute to 

distorted price signals, suppressing real-time prices, and affecting revenue adequacy.  Variable 

energy resources are generally able to produce close to a forecasted amount.  Therefore, the 

MMU continues to recommend that SPP and its stakeholders address this issue through market 

incentives and rule changes that focus on market inefficiencies associated with under-clearing of 

variable energy resources in the day-ahead market based on forecasted supply.  These rule 

changes could focus on changing incentives for wind resources, or alternatively encouraging 

virtual transactions.

downward trend.

average

In 2023, 19 of 70 asset owners (27 percent) with load experienced negative prices in more than 

15 percent of intervals.  This is a decrease from 2022, where 54 percent of asset owners 

experienced negative prices in excess of 15 percent of intervals.  The asset owner with the 

highest percentage of intervals with negative prices in 2023 had 29 percent of intervals with 

negative prices; this is down from 2022 when the highest percentage was 32 percent of all 

intervals. 

The MMU remains concerned about the frequency of negative price intervals.  Negative prices 

may not be a problem in and of themselves, however, they do indicate an increase in surplus 

energy on the system.  This is exacerbated by the practice of self-committing of resources and 

manual commitments for capacity.  In the SPP market where there is an abundance of capacity 

and significant levels of renewable resources, negative prices can occur when renewable 

resources need to be backed down in order for traditional resources to meet their committed 

generation.  Moreover, unit commitment differences, due to under-clearing of wind resources in 

the day-ahead market and then producing more in the real-time market, can create differences 

in the frequency of negative price intervals between the day-ahead and real-time markets.  This 

disparity between the markets negatively impacts the efficient commitment of resources.   

As more wind generation and other renewables are anticipated to be added over the coming 

years, the frequency of negative prices has the potential to increase.  Negative price intervals in 

the day-ahead highlight the need for changes in market rules to address self-committing of 

State of the Market 2023 

151 

 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 4 
Market prices and costs 

resources in the day-ahead market and addressing differences in supply between day-ahead 

and real time.  These issues are discussed further in Chapter 7. 

  OPERATING RESERVE MARKET PRICES  

Operating reserve is made up of four products:  (1) regulation-up, (2) regulation-down, (3) 

spinning reserve, and (4) supplemental reserve.  The regulation products are used to ensure the 

amount of generation matches load on a subinterval basis.  Generators respond to regulation 

instructions in seconds.  Spinning and supplemental products are reserved for contingency 

situations and respond to instructions within ten minutes. 

In addition, regulating units are compensated for mileage costs incurred when moving from one 

set point instruction to another.  The market calculates a mileage factor for both products each 

month that represents the percentage a unit is expected to be deployed compared to what it 

cleared.  If a unit is deployed more than the expected percentage, then the unit is entitled to 

reimbursement for the excess at the regulation mileage marginal price.  If the unit is deployed 

less, it must buy back its position at the real-time mileage clearing price. 

Generally speaking, regulation-up and regulation-down usually have the highest market clearing 

prices.  Supplemental reserves always have the lowest average prices of the operating reserve 

products, with prices typically averaging less than two dollars on an annual basis.  There has 

been a general upward trend in operating reserve product prices over the past three years.  

Day-ahead and real-time price patterns vary for regulation-up and regulation-down, see Figure 

4–20 and Figure 4–21.  The dots shown on the charts represent prices for 2021 without February, 

thus showing the trend compared to a more normal year.

Much of the decrease in regulation-up and regulation-down prices in 2023 can be attributed to 

lower gas prices during the year. 

During a 2017 review of SPP’s regulation products, the MMU identified two issues with the 

regulation mileage calculations and settlement.  First, regulation down mileage prices were often 

inflated due to the mileage prices being set by the highest cleared mileage offer.  Second, 

expected mileage megawatts were too high, causing increased buyback costs for participants 
clearing unused mileage. 107  SPP has brought forward two design changes to correct these 
issues.108  The changes have been approved by FERC and are expected to be implemented in 

June 2024.  The MMU is in support of both changes and expects to see a reduction in both 

unused mileage buy back costs and regulation down mileage price spikes.

Day-ahead supplemental reserve prices climbed from $1.81/MW in 2022 to $2.01/MW in 2023

4.2  UPLIFT 

The Integrated Marketplace provides make-whole payments to generators to ensure that the 

market provides sufficient revenue to cover the cleared offers providing energy and operating 

reserves for a period in which the resource was committed.  To preserve the incentive for a 

resource to meet its market commitment and dispatch instruction, market payments should 

cover the sum of the incremental energy cost, start-up cost, no-load cost, transition cost, and 

cost of operating reserve products.  The make-whole payment provides additional market 

payments in cases where revenue is below a resource’s offer to make the resource whole to its 

offer. 

For the resources that are not combined-cycle, settlements separately evaluate:  (1) day-ahead 

market commitments based on day-ahead market prices and cleared offers; and (2) reliability 

unit commitments based on real-time market prices and cleared operating reserve offers.  

Combined-cycle resources that registered as multi-configuration resources are unique in that 

they can be cleared in both the day-ahead and real-time markets at the same time.  As a result, 

settlements must evaluate the revenues and cost of both real-time and day-ahead commitments 

when calculating real-time make-whole payments for combined-cycles.

Because of the 2021 February winter weather event, make-whole payments climbed to levels 

well beyond those ever experienced in the Integrated Marketplace, thus February totals are 

removed from the charts above.  For 2023, day-ahead market and reliability unit commitment 

make-whole payments combined totaled $236 million.  Reliability unit commitment make-whole 

payments constituted 66 percent of the total make-whole payments in 2022.  

Day-ahead make-whole payments were nearly $79 million in 2023, down from $172 million in 

2022, a decrease of 54 percent.  Reliability unit commitment make-whole payments in 2023 

totaled $157 million, down from $292 million in 2022, representing a 47 percent decrease.  

State of the Market 2023 

157 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 4 
Market prices and costs 

Much of the higher level of make-whole payments in 2022 can be attributed to higher gas prices 

during the year. 

2023 saw a marked increase in make-whole payments to the ‘other’ resource category, which 

primarily consists of fuel oil units.  With high net loads experienced during late August due to 

high temperatures across the footprint, increased make-whole payments were allocated to fuel 

oil units due to them being committed for reliability during this period. 

Some causes of make-whole payments included, but are not limited to:  local reliability 

commitments, uncaptured congestion in the day-ahead market, under-cleared generation 

(primarily wind) in the day-ahead market, inflexibility of resources to move in economic ranges 

or go offline between on-peak and off-peak hours, and excessive transmission congestion not 

being solved by the market. 

Total make-whole payments per megawatt generated averaged $0.83/MWh for 2023, up from 

$0.08/MWh for 2022, but down from $5.83/MWh in 2021, which does include February.  Self-

committed resources are not eligible for make-whole payment reimbursements.  Primarily, 

resources committed under reliability or market status are eligible for cost reimbursement.  Due 

to the effects of the winter weather event in 2021, 2021 make-whole payments were much 

higher.   

Figure 4–27 illustrates the 2021 to 2023 average make-whole payments per each megawatt 

eligible for cost reimbursement, or in other words those megawatts generated under market or 

reliability status.  These include manual commitments made by operators.

and real-time were significantly lower than 2022, primarily due to higher natural gas prices in 

2022.   

Operators often commit resources when the available ramp capacity needs in future intervals is 

perceived to be short.  These actions often reduce the occurrence of scarcity events.  However, 

this has the effect of potentially suppressing the price signal that would indicate a problem as 

capacity is brought on to meet the perceived ramp shortage.  Manual commitments can and 

sometimes do lead to make-whole payments.  Commitments that come from the security-

constrained economic commitment are preferred.  Additionally, capacity committed manually 

can often lead to price suppression.  Another way to view the real-time make-whole payments is 

that on average $27.58/MWh was paid to avoid reliability problems that were not able to be 

addressed directly by the real-time market. 

In addition, most scarcity events last less than two intervals and most resources have start times 

that are longer than this period and minimum-run times that are much longer than this period.  

This means that even if these resources are able to capture one to two intervals of the high 

prices, they may have to run an hour or two longer with less economic price levels, leading to 

the need for cost reimbursement.   

The MMU believed that the ramp capability product (see Section 3.2.4), which went into 

production in March 2022, the uncertainty product (see Section 3.2.5) implemented in July 2023, 

and fast-start resource pricing logic, should help provide the appropriate pricing and 

compensation mechanisms for meeting ramp capacity needs in the market.  However, both the 

ramp capability product and the uncertainty product need to be enhanced and improved to 

provide maximum benefit to the market.  This includes reducing make-whole-payments and 

bringing transparency to the market.  Enhancements to these products will also improve price 

formation and better compensate resources that provide the much-needed ramping flexibility, 

as well as reduce the need for manual commitments for capacity.

In recent years, there has been a large increase in make-whole payments occurring during 

periods that resources are manually committed for capacity.  In fact, just under 23 percent of the 

real-time make-whole payments were paid to resources committed manually for capacity needs.  

This is up from 14 percent in 2022, but lower than 39 percent in 2021.  However, capacity 

commitments were much higher in 2021 during the winter weather event, leading to this 

increase.  There was a decrease in resources committed for transmission from 2022, but above 

2021, which was lower due to the winter weather.  These commitments had 19 percent of the 

real-time make whole payments in 2023, down from 29 percent in 2022, but up from 10 percent 

in 2021. 

Make-whole payments associated with voltage support commitments do not follow the same 

uplift process outlined in Section 4.3.1.  Instead, the cost of these make-whole payments is 

distributed to the settlement areas that benefited from the commitment by way of a load ratio 

share.  Figure 4–29 illustrates the level of make-whole payments associated with voltage support 

commitments.

In 2023, there were 10 market participants that each received annual make-whole payments in

  MAKE-WHOLE PAYMENT ALLOCATION 

The allocation of both day-ahead and real-time make-whole payments has important 

consequences to the market.  In principle, for market efficiency purposes uplift cost allocation 

should be directed to those members that contributed to the need for the make-whole 

payments (i.e., cost causation).   

For the day-ahead market, make-whole payment costs are distributed to both physical and 

virtual withdrawals on a per-MWh rate.  The per-MWh rate is derived by dividing the sum of all 

day-ahead make-whole payments for an operating day by the sum of all cleared day-ahead 

market load megawatts, export megawatts, and virtual bids for the operating day.  The average 

per-MWh rate for withdrawing locations in the day-ahead market was $0.26/MWh for 2023; 

down from the 2022 rate of $0.55/MWh, and in line with the 2021 rate at $0.29/MWh.   

For the real-time market, make-whole payment costs are distributed through a per-MWh rate 

that is assigned to all megawatt-hours of deviation in the real-time market.  The 2023 per-MWh 

rate was $2.35/MWh, down substantially from the $3.67/MWh in 2022, but up from the 2021 

rate of $1.81/MWh.  There are eight categories of deviation and each category receives an equal 

amount per megawatt, which can vary by operating day, when the cost of make-whole 

payments is applied.

Even though each category of deviation is applied the same rate for deviation, approximately 85 

percent (settlement location deviation in the table above) of the real-time make-whole payment 

costs were paid by entities withdrawing (physical or virtual) more megawatts in the real-time 

market than the day-ahead market.  

Transactions susceptible to this charge are virtual offer megawatts; real-time load megawatts 

different from the day-ahead megawatts cleared; import, export, and through megawatts in 

real-time different from the megawatts cleared in the day-ahead market; and units pulling 

substation power different from any megawatts produced by the unit.  However, virtual offers 

are the most susceptible as 100 percent of their megawatts are considered incremental.  

Because of this, virtual offers alone paid 48 percent of all real-time make-whole payments in 

2023, down from 53 percent in 2022.  Historically, virtual transactions have paid around 50 

percent of real-time make-whole payments annually. 

Cost causation has been an area of concern in the SPP working groups in the past few years.  In 

particular, participants raised concerns that the market is not properly allocating the market cost 

back to those responsible for causing those costs.  With virtual offers bearing such a heavy 

burden of these costs, it reduces the incentives for behavior changes among those that are 

causing the cost, and it adds a premium to virtual transactions.  This should be considered as 

State of the Market 2023 

163 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 4 
Market prices and costs 

part of the evaluation of under-clearing of wind in the day-ahead as these incentives likely 

contribute to the lack of price convergence between day-ahead and real-time. 

  REGULATION MILEAGE MAKE-WHOLE PAYMENTS 

In March 2015, SPP introduced regulation compensation changes for units deployed for 

regulation-up and regulation-down.  One component of the regulation compensation charges is 

regulation-up and regulation-down mileage make-whole payments for units that are charged 

for unused regulation-up or regulation-down mileage at a rate that is in excess of the 

regulation-up or regulation-down mileage offer.   

SPP calculates mileage factors monthly for both regulation-up and regulation-down.  These 

mileage factors are ratios of historical averages of the percentage of each regulation product 

deployed to the regulation product cleared in the prior month.  The regulation-up mileage 

factor averaged 14 percent in 2023, down from 16 percent in 2022, while the regulation-down 

mileage factor averaged 18 percent in 2023, up from 22 percent in 2022. 

The mileage factor is a key component in the computation of mileage make-whole payments.  

When the mileage factor is greater than the percentage of deployed regulating megawatts to 

cleared regulating megawatts for each product, the resource must buy back the non-deployed 

megawatts at the mileage marginal clearing price for the respective product.  If the mileage 

marginal clearing price used for the buyback is greater than the unit’s cost for the product a 

make-whole payment may be granted. 

Figure 4–33, below, illustrates the mileage make-whole payments for 2023 and the prior two 

years.

Regulation-up mileage make-whole payments were around $400,000 in 2023, down 29 percent

  DISTRIBUTION OF MARGINAL LOSSES (OVER -COLLECTED 

LOSS REVENUE) 

Both the congestion and loss components of prices create excess revenues for SPP that must be 

distributed to market participants in an economically efficient manner.  In the case of marginal 

loss revenues, this requires that the distribution does not alter market incentives.   

The current design consolidates the distributions of day-ahead and real-time over-collected loss 
rebates into one distribution.110  Both day-ahead and real-time over-collected loss rebates are 

distributed on only real-time withdrawing megawatts.  This includes loads, substation power, 

exports, wheel-throughs, pseudo-ties, and bilateral settlement schedules (BSS).  The only 

exception is that both day-ahead and real-time bilateral settlement schedules are entitled to the 

110 Prior years over-collected loss designs are described in the 2018 Annual State of the Market report 
under Section 4.2.3.

rebate, as long as the underlying megawatts associated to the bilateral settlement schedules are 

not less than the megawatts of the bilateral settlement schedule.

  REVENUE NEUTRALITY UPLIFT 

Revenue neutrality uplift (RNU), shown in Figure 4–35, ensures settlement payments and 

receipts for each hourly settlement interval equal zero.  Positive revenue neutrality uplift 

indicates that SPP receives insufficient revenue and collects from market participants.  Negative 

revenue-neutrality uplift indicates where SPP receives excess revenue, which must be credited 

back to market participants.

Total monthly revenue neutrality uplift for 2023 was $205 million, down 62 percent from $546 

million in 2022.  The two main components of revenue neutrality uplift are real-time congestion 

and real-time joint operating agreement (also known as market-to-market).  The joint operating 

agreement component generally acts to lessen revenue neutrality uplift, while the congestion 

component generally acts to increase revenue neutrality uplift.  Real-time joint operating 

agreement portion of revenue neutrality uplift was $94 million in 2023, a decrease of 43 percent 

from $165 million in 2022.  The real-time congestion component of revenue neutrality uplift was 

$283 million in 2023, a decrease of 57 percent from $654 million in 2022.  The high level of day-

ahead and real-time revenue inadequacy portion in 2021 was a result of the February winter 

weather event. 

The increase in the real-time congestion component of revenue neutrality uplift has been the 

topic of much discussion during 2022.  At the February 2023 Market Working Group meeting, 
SPP staff reported111 on the increased congestion component.  SPP staff stated that overall, as 

real-time congestion increases, the congestion imbalance between day-ahead and real-time 

drives up revenue neutrality uplift.

Real-time production cost decreased by 43 percent from 2021 (including all months) to 2023 to

million and $45 million.   

4.4  LONG-RUN PRICE SIGNALS FOR INVESTMENT 

In the long term, market prices provide signals for investment in new transmission and 

generation, as well as ongoing maintenance of existing generation and transmission assets to 

meet load.  Given the relatively low average SPP market prices since the beginning of the 

market, the MMU does not expect SPP market prices by themselves to support new entry of 

generation investments.  While the SPP market on its own has historically offered low incentives 

for new generation, some reasons for new generation investments include expansion of 

corporate renewable goals, bilateral contracts, purchase power agreements, SPP market 

protocol requirements, federal and/or state incentives, state-regulated investments, emerging 

technologies, and emission reduction plans.  However, stakeholder reactions and feedback to 

SPP’s Board of Directors 2022 approval of a 3-percentage point increase in the Planning Reserve 

Margin (PRM) suggests that these out-of-market factors are not significant enough to drive 

enough investment in new generation to keep up with SPP’s reliability needs. 

State of the Market 2023 

170 

 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 4 
Market prices and costs 

To determine the extent market prices could support new investments, the MMU analyzed the 

fixed costs, and annual fixed operating and maintenance costs of five generation technologies 
relative to their potential net revenues117 at SPP market prices.  The generator types include 

scrubbed coal, natural gas combined-cycle, industrial frame natural gas combustion turbine, 

wind, and solar photovoltaic with storage.  This analysis uses daily averages and assumptions to 

estimate profitability of constructing new generation and supporting the on-going cost of a new 

generator.  It does not reflect profitability of any specific existing generator.  Many costs, such as 

supply adequacy penalties and individual contracts, are not included in this analysis. 

This is a very high-level analysis that uses several assumptions and averages.  Daily averages at a 

hub were used for gas prices.  A single gas hub was assumed for each region.  These gas prices 

did not account for bilateral trades, hedges or any additional charges not represented by the 

hub price.  Energy prices used were daily unweighted averages across the footprint.   

Figure 4–39 provides the cost assumptions for a new generator.  A capital recovery factor of 12.6 

percent was used in the annual fixed operating and maintenance cost component.

SPP market revenues have been insufficient to support the cost of new entry of all generation 

types since the inception of the Integrated Marketplace in 2014 through 2020.  The winter 

weather event in 2021 drove revenues to the point where the cost of new entry for natural gas 

combined cycle and combustion turbines may have been met.  In 2022, revenues were only 

sufficient to cover the cost of new entry for wind capacity.  In 2023, the trend of insufficient 

revenues to meet the cost of new entry requirements continued across all major capacity types, 

at least on a regional basis.  However, whether revenues would support new investment more 

broadly may not be as clear given specific differences in revenues and costs.  The analysis 

supporting Figure 4-41 is a market-wide analysis and does not account for regional and 

resource-specific factors influencing margin such as fuel storage, fuel contracts, etc.

Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 4 
Market prices and costs 

Overwhelmingly, the conclusions do not vary geographically, despite differing energy prices and 

fuel costs.  Historically, the SPS region has had higher net revenues than other regions for gas 

resources.  This is likely because combined-cycle plants and combustion turbines in the Permian 

Basin (West Texas) region consistently experience below average natural gas prices.  

In 2022, net revenues across the RTO only supported new wind capacity builds.  While 2023 net 

revenues across SPP did not meet the cost of new entry for any of the generation types 

analyzed, the regional break-down of the data in Figure 4-42 indicates that natural gas and 

combined-cycle units in the Western Area Power Administration’s Upper Great Plains East 

(WAUE) would have received sufficient net revenues to cover their respective cost of new entry.  

As is discussed in detail in Chapter 5, this was largely driven by a new commercial load coming 

on-line in February 2023 which had a significant impact on congestion and associated prices.  

However, new transmission infrastructure and upgrades to existing facilities scheduled to be 

online by 2025 could mitigate the relative high prices in the region on a going forward basis. 

Based on these results, the MMU expects the market to continue to signal the retirement of 

some coal generation while also not signaling the long-term investment of other types of new 

generation.  A decrease in overall available capacity—along with observed higher outages—and 

changes in the generation fleet profile could present challenges for reliability. 

External economic decisions can provide additional impetus needed for new generation 

investments, such as the expansion of corporate renewable goals, SPP out-of-market payments, 

bilateral contracts, purchase power agreements, SPP market protocol requirements, federal 

and/or state incentives, state-regulated investments, emerging technologies, and/or emission 

reduction plans.  However, market prices, by themselves, have not historically signaled new 

generation entry since the inception of the Integrated Marketplace.  Other revenue streams for 

value added could change this conclusion.  For instance, resources could be paid to ensure their 

generation is highly dependable, or resources could be paid to remain available for specific 

performance requirements in the short to medium timeframe.  As the market is currently 

designed, it does not incentivize new entry for energy capacity.   

Out-of-market actions by SPP operators, and the resultant make-whole payments, for reliability 

(manual) commitments reflect some of the symptomatic issues.  If these make-whole payments 

were represented in a market price for energy or ancillary services, then the out-of-market 

payments would likely decrease, and market-based prices would likely increase.  The increase in 

market prices would support net revenue for all capacity resources with marginal costs below 

that of the marginal unit[s] setting price in any interval.  In terms of magnitude, day-ahead and 

real-time uplift payments have historically only accounted for roughly 4 percent of the all-in cost 

State of the Market 2023 

174 

 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 4 
Market prices and costs 

of cost of energy122 which is generally far less than the historical shortages of net revenue 

relative to the cost of new entry for various technology types.  This suggests that market policies 

which maximize the ability of operators to minimize uplift in lieu of better price formation in the 

markets would, by themselves, not solve the money gap needed to incent new generation 

builds. 

Continuing to value availability and flexibility will be important going forward.  Compensation 

for reliable accreditation and performance could be an additional revenue stream to the high-

performing generation.  This is discussed in more detail in Chapter 7 of this report.  The 

effectiveness of the pricing of such products and capacity ratings to align reliability and 

economics will shape future price signals for investment in the SPP market.

reserves day-ahead uplift and real-time uplift.

5  CONGESTION AND TRANSMISSION CONGESTION 

RIGHTS MARKET 

This chapter reviews transmission congestion in the SPP market footprint, as well as the 

transmission congestion rights market in the Integrated Marketplace.  Key points from this 

chapter include: 

•  The areas that experienced the highest congestion costs in 2023 were the northwest 

corner of North Dakota and southeastern Oklahoma, as well as a concentrated area in 

southeast North Dakota near the MISO seam.  Much of Kansas, Nebraska, South Dakota, 

and northwest Oklahoma experienced the lowest congestion costs for the year. 

•  Other than a highly congested area in northwest North Dakota, congestion patterns 

were similar to the previous year in the SPP footprint.  However, price splits were smaller 

on the southeastern corner of the SPP footprint where there is a boundary between 

inexpensive renewable and conventional generation.  Intervals having no congestion are 

again rare in day-ahead in 2023 but more than doubled in real-time.  Intervals having a 

breached constraint decreased in 2023 with just over half of the intervals breached in the 

real-time market. 

•  The 2023 frequently constrained area study identified the Joplin, Missouri; Tulsa, 

Oklahoma; Oklahoma City, Oklahoma; and Williston, North Dakota areas as frequently 

constrained areas and these areas continued to see elevated congestion prices during 

2023. 

• 

In aggregate, load-serving entities covered 149 percent of their congestion cost, non-

load-serving entities only covered 66 percent of their total congestion cost. 

• 

Individual market participants hedged congestion with varying degrees of effectiveness.  

Overall 75 percent of load-serving entities recovered all of their congestion cost.  

•  Total congestion payments for 2023 were nearly $1.4 billion; down from the $2 billion in 

2022 but up from $1.2 billion in 2021.  The decrease seen in 2023 can be mostly 

attributed to reductions in fuel supply costs.  

•  Transmission congestion rights funding fell outside the target range of 90 percent again, 

coming in at 88 percent.  While the funding percentage stayed the same as last year, the 

annual shortfall decreased by $112 million. 

State of the Market 2023 

177 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 5 
Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

•  Auction revenue right funding decreased from 122 percent to 118 percent.  Relatedly, 

the ARR surplus decreased by more than $40 million year over year. 

•  Participants can transfer congestion rights through use of a bulletin board or sell back 

positions in the auction.  However, most congestion rights are not transferred or sold.  
Intra-auction sales123 increased slightly in volume and averaged just over 6.3 percent of 

the total auction volume.  Bulletin board trades amounted to less than one percent of 

the total auction volume cleared during 2023.   

5.1  TRANSMISSION CONGESTION  

The locational marginal price (LMP) for approximately 1,200 settlement locations in the SPP 

market reflects the sum of three components: 

1)   marginal energy component (MEC) - system-wide marginal cost of the energy 

required to serve the market, 

2)   marginal congestion component (MCC) - the marginal cost of any increase or 

decrease in energy at a location with respect to transmission constraints, and 

3)   marginal loss component (MLC) - the marginal cost of any increase or decrease in 

energy to minimize system transmission losses. 

𝐿𝑀𝑃 = 𝑀𝐸𝐶 + 𝑀𝐶𝐶 + 𝑀𝐿𝐶 

LMPs are a key feature of electricity markets that ensure the efficient scheduling, commitment, 

and dispatch of generation given the system load and reliability constraints.  LMPs also provide 

price signals for efficient incentives for future generation and transmission investment and help 

guide retirement decisions.   

This section focuses on the congestion and loss components of price and related items 

including: 

•  geographic pattern of congestion and losses, 

•  changes in the transmission system that alter congestion patterns, 

•  congestion impacts on local market power, 

• 

load-serving entities hedging congestion costs in the transmission congestion rights 

market, and 

123 The sale of a previously acquired position in a subsequent auction.

Market Monitoring Unit

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

•  distribution of marginal congestion and loss amounts. 

  PRICING PATTERNS AND CONGESTION    

Figure 5–1 shows price contour maps representing the day-ahead and real-time average prices 

in 2023. 

Figure 5–1  Price map, day-ahead and real-time market 

Annual average day-ahead market prices ranged from around $12/MWh in west Kansas to 

$95/MWh in the northwest section of North Dakota.  Almost 60 percent of the 1,200 settlement 

locations had an annual average day-ahead market price between $20/MWh and $30/MWh.  

Congestion accounted for about 89 percent of the price variation in 2023, which surpassed 85 

percent in 2022.  This is the highest percentage in price variation due to congestion since the 

beginning of the Integrated Marketplace in 2014.  Marginal losses accounted for 11 percent of 

the price variation in 2023 compared to 15 percent in 2022.  Because congestion is more volatile 

in the real-time market, the average geographic price range is slightly larger, from $5/MWh to 

$102/MWh. 

State of the Market 2023 

179 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool, Inc. 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Chapter 5 
Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

Transmission buildout has allowed higher levels of low-cost wind generation in the western 

parts of the SPP footprint to serve load centers located in the eastern portions of SPP.  

Congestion remains on the southeastern edge of SPP extending from northern Missouri to 

south Oklahoma which signifies the boundary between inexpensive renewable and conventional 

generation.  Concentrated congestion is more prevalent in the northwest and east sections of 

North Dakota and in southeast Oklahoma.   

The southwest Missouri124 area along the SPP eastern border continued to see congestion but 

average real-time prices decreased from around $69/MWh in 2022 to around $30/MWh in 2023.  

Another area along the SPP border that continues to see consistent congestion is southeast 
Oklahoma.125  Prices in this area remain elevated with an average real-time price of $46/MWh in 

2023.  The highest average real-time prices in 2023 were in a concentrated area in northwest 
North Dakota around an SPP market-to-market constraint126 that averaged $102/MWh.  

  CONGESTION BY GEOGRAPHIC LOCATION  

The major drivers of the congestion pattern in SPP are the physical characteristics of the 

transmission grid and associated transfer capability, the geographic distribution of load, the 

geographic differences in fuel costs, and external flows from neighboring areas.  The eastern 

side of the SPP footprint, with a higher concentration of load, also has a higher concentration of 

high-voltage (345 kV) transmission lines.  Historically, high-voltage connections between the 

west and east have been limited but transmission buildout has resulted in most congestion 

occurring on the southeastern edge of the SPP footprint.   

The cost of coal-fired generation increases as transportation costs rise.  For example, 

transportation cost increases with distance from the Wyoming Powder River Basin near the 

northwest corner of SPP’s footprint.  This is important because even though it is declining, coal 

still accounts for 23 percent of SPP’s installed capacity and 27 percent energy generation in 

2023. 

Natural gas-fired generation, SPP’s largest fuel type by installed capacity (37 percent in 2023), 

resides predominantly in the southern portions of SPP.  Wind-powered generation generally lies

in the western half of the footprint, and nuclear generation resides near the center, while the 

majority of hydro is located in the north.   

These factors combine to create a general northwest to southeast split in prices.  The split, or 

difference, in prices between these two areas was substantially less in 2023 compared to 2022 

and 2021 reflecting a decrease in congestion.  Exceptions were high prices in northwest North 
Dakota and eastern North Dakota along the MISO seam near a MISO constraint.127  

Additionally, following approximately 220 MW of new commercial load added to the northwest 

portion of North Dakota in February 2023, the area has observed high prices and significant 
levels of congestion in both the day-ahead and real-time markets.128  Generation in the area has 

been at higher than normal levels in order to meet the newly introduced load.  Meanwhile, 

several resources key to relieving the constraint reached their Environmental Protection Agency 

(EPA) emission limits in late July forcing them to go into reliability and/or outage status.  This 
created difficulties in meeting demand and controlling congestion in the area.129   

Along with this development, transmission congestion rights funding is heavily impacted by the 

day-ahead congestion on the Charlie Creek to Watford City constraint.  This resulted in over 

nine times the underfunding seen on the next most underfunded constraint during the period of 

June through September.  Because the SPP and MISO footprints are highly interwoven across 

this area, both markets experience parallel flows across each other’s transmission system.  The 

current joint operating agreements between SPP and MISO are intended to manage these 

market-to-market constraints through coordinated dispatch and settle based on transmission 

rights. 

Two transmission projects, Roundup to Kummer Ridge and Finstad to Tande to Leland Olds, are 

being built that should help reduce the congestion, however, they are not expected to be 

operational before 2025.  A 600 MW addition of resource capacity is also planned in the area by 

Basin Electric Power Cooperative whose first phase of the build is also not expected to be 

completed until 2025.  A remedial action scheme was implemented in March 2024 to mitigate 

congestion on the Charlie Creek to Watford City constraint.
